Generalized cluster states from Hopf algebras: non-invertible symmetry and Hopf tensor network representation
Zhian Jia

Abstract
Cluster states are crucial resources for measurement-based quantum computation (MBQC). It exhibits symmetry-protected topological (SPT) order, thus also playing a crucial role in studying topological phases. We present the construction of cluster states based on Hopf algebras. By generalizing the finite group valued qudit to a Hopf algebra valued qudit and introducing the generalized Pauli-X operator based on the regular action of the Hopf algebra, as well as the generalized Pauli-Z operator based on the irreducible representation action on the Hopf algebra, we develop a comprehensive theory of Hopf qudits. We demonstrate that non-invertible symmetry naturally emerges for Hopf qudits. Subsequently, for a bipartite graph termed the cluster graph, we assign the identity state and trivial representation state to even and odd vertices, respectively. Introducing the edge entangler as controlled regular action, we provide a general construction of Hopf cluster states. To ensure the commutativity of the edge entangler, we propose a method to construct a cluster lattice for any triangulable manifold. We use the 1d cluster state as an example to illustrate our construction. As this serves as a promising candidate for SPT phases, we construct the gapped Hamiltonian for this scenario and provide a detailed discussion of its non-invertible symmetries. We demonstrate that the 1d cluster state model is equivalent to the quasi-1d Hopf quantum double model with one rough boundary and one smooth boundary. We also discuss the generalization of the Hopf cluster state model to the Hopf ladder model through symmetry topological field theory. Furthermore, we introduce the Hopf tensor network representation of Hopf cluster states by integrating the tensor representation of structure constants with the string diagrams of the Hopf algebra, which can be used to solve the Hopf cluster state model.



1
Introduction

Quantum computation, with the potential to revolutionize computation by solving certain tasks that are classically intractable, has garnered considerable attention over the past several decades. It remains one of the main challenges in the development of modern quantum technologies
Nielsen and Chuang (2010)
;
Preskill (1998)
.
The standard model for quantum computation is the quantum circuit model
Deutsch (1989)
which acts both as a framework for theoretical investigations and as a guide for experiments.
The measurement-based, or one-way, model of quantum computation (MBQC) provides a crucial alternative
Raussendorf and Briegel (2001)
;
Raussendorf et al. (2003)
;
Nielsen (2006)
;
Briegel et al. (2009)
. MBQC utilizes highly entangled states, commonly referred to as graph states (sometimes called cluster states by certain authors). In this work, we adopt the term ‘cluster graph’ to denote a bipartite graph with specific additional properties (see Definition
2
). Quantum computing operations are accomplished by performing measurements on these states.

The definition of a qubit graph state is simple.
For a graph
K=(V,E)
, a qubit graph state is uniquely determined by the underlying graph
K

Raussendorf and Briegel (2001)
;
Raussendorf et al. (2003)
;
Nielsen (2006)
;
Briegel et al. (2009)
:

|K,\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\rangle=(\prod_{k=1}^{|E|}CZ_{e_{k}})(\otimes_{i=1}^{|V|}|+\rangle_{v_{i}}),

(1)

where
CZ
is controlled-
Z
gate.
The stabilizers are
T_{v}=X_{v}\otimes(\otimes_{w\in N(v)}Z_{w})
with
N(v)
denoting the set of vertices that are neighbours of
v
.
See Ref.
Hein et al. (2006)
for a thorough review of the qubit graph state.
There exist a variety of generalizations of the qubit graph state, for example, hypergraph states
Qu et al. (2013)
;
Rossi et al. (2013)
; qudit graph and hypergraph state
Steinhoff et al. (2017)
;
Xiong et al. (2018)
;
Cui et al. (2015)
; cluster graph state based on finite group
Brell (2015)
;
Fechisin et al. (2023)
; and graph and hypergraph state for continuous variables
Walschaers et al. (2018)
;
Moore (2019)
.
In addition to its application in MBQC, these highly entangled states also find utility in quantum teleportation, quantum metrology, quantum error-correction, quantum secret sharing, and various other quantum information tasks
Hein et al. (2006)
;
Qu et al. (2013)
;
Rossi et al. (2013)
;
Steinhoff et al. (2017)
;
Xiong et al. (2018)
;
Cui et al. (2015)
;
Fechisin et al. (2023)
;
Walschaers et al. (2018)
;
Moore (2019)
;
Looi et al. (2008)
;
Markham and Sanders (2008)
;
Keet et al. (2010)
, among others mentioned in the literature.

From a quantum matter perspective, the one dimensional cluster state is a typical example of symmetry-protected topological (SPT) order.
The Hamiltonian corresponds to Eq. (
1
) is

H_{\rm cluster}=-\sum_{i}Z_{i-1}X_{i}Z_{i+1}.

(2)

This model has the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SPT order
Son et al. (2012)
. Recently, it’s also argued that the cluster state model also has a non-invertible global symmetry, described by the fusion category
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})

Seifnashri and Shao (2024)
.
As the qubit system is a quantum system valued in the Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, it is natural to consider the generalization to the finite group case. In this scenario, the Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS)-type formulation of the graph state for bipartite graphs must be utilized
Brell (2015)
.
After applying Hadamard gates over even vertices, we obtain the CSS qubit cluster state:

\displaystyle|K,\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]\rangle_{\rm CSS}=(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle u,v\rangle\\
\\
u\in V_{o},v\in V_{e}\end{subarray}}CX_{\langle u,v\rangle})[(\otimes_{u\in V_{o}}|+\rangle_{u})\otimes(\otimes_{v\in V_{e}}|0\rangle_{v})],

(3)

where we have used
\langle u,v\rangle
to represent adjacent vertices (an edge).
The stabilizer for this state becomes
X_{i-1}X_{i}X_{i+1}
for odd
i
and
Z_{i-1}Z_{i}Z_{i+1}
for even
i
, which is of the CSS-type. The corresponding cluster state Hamiltonian is

H=-\sum_{i\in 2\mathbb{N}}Z_{i-1}Z_{i}Z_{i+1}-\sum_{i\in 2\mathbb{N}+1}X_{i-1}X_{i}X_{i+1}

(4)

Based on this observation, Brell generalizes the cluster state by using the finite group valued qudit
Brell (2015)
and generalized form of Pauli operators, akin to Kitaev’s quantum double model
Kitaev (2003)
;
Albert et al. (2021)
.
The non-invertible symmetry and SPT order for finite group cluster state are recently discussed in Ref.
Fechisin et al. (2023)
.
Via using tensor network representation, they show that for finite group
G
valued cluster state, there is a
G\times\mathsf{Rep}(G)
non-invertible symmetry.

However, Hopf algebras provide a more general framework for studying non-generalized symmetries. Roughly speaking, generalized global symmetries can be classified as invertible and non-invertible symmetries. The invertible symmetries are characterized by groups, while non-invertible symmetries are usually characterized by categories or other algebraic structures, where the elements are generally non-invertible. For more details, see, e.g., Refs.
Cordova et al. (2022)
;
Brennan and Hong (2023)
;
McGreevy (2023)
;
Luo et al. (2024)
;
Shao (2024)
;
Schäfer-Nameki (2024)
;
Bhardwaj et al. (2024a)
;
Delcamp and Tiwari (2024)
. Typical examples of non-invertible symmetries include fusion category symmetries
Fröhlich et al. (2004)
;
Fuchs et al. (2007)
;
Fröhlich et al. (2010)
;
Bhardwaj and Tachikawa (2018)
;
Chang et al. (2019)
;
Thorngren and Wang (2024)
;
Thorngren and Wang (2021)
;
Komargodski et al. (2021)
;
Inamura (2022)
;
Inamura (2023)
.
Depending on the codimension of the topological defect on which the symmetry operator is defined, there can be
0
-form and higher-form symmetries
Gaiotto et al. (2015)
;
Kapustin and Thorngren (2017)
;
Gomes (2023)
;
Bhardwaj et al. (2024a)
. Constructing lattice models that exhibit non-invertible symmetries and higher-form symmetries has attracted significant attention in recent years
Inamura (2022)
;
Inamura (2023)
;
Bhardwaj et al. (2024b)
;
Bhardwaj et al. (2024c)
.
Since cluster state models have non-invertible symmetry, building on this line of inquiry, the authors in Ref.
Fechisin et al. (2023)
pose the open problem of constructing the Hopf algebra cluster state and exploring its SPT order. The main objective of this work is to present a general construction of Hopf algebra valued cluster states and develop their tensor network representation. Discussions regarding SPT order will be addressed elsewhere
Jia ()
.

Similar to that for finite group case, we find the Hopf cluster state is closely related to the Hopf quantum double model
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Yan et al. (2022)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
.
The Haar integral of the Hopf algebra plays a crucial role in the Hopf quantum double model, inspired by this, we use the Haar integral as a preferred input state.
We emphasize the significance of coalgebra and antipode structures for constructing a Hopf algebra valued cluster state.
The generalized Pauli-Z operators are defined based on representations of the Hopf algebra, while the generalized Pauli-X operators are defined using regular actions of the Hopf algebra on itself. The edge entangler is defined by a generalized controlled-X operation, which heavily relies on the comultiplication of the Hopf algebra.
The following are our main contributions:
(i) We introduce the Hopf algebra-valued qudit and outline the construction of generalized Pauli operators in this scenario. Building upon this foundation, we proceed to construct the Hopf cluster states.
(ii) We introduce a Hopf tensor network that combines Hopf algebra structure constants with string diagrams of the Hopf algebra. We provide a detailed discussion of the Hopf tensor network representation of the Hopf cluster state.
(iii) We delve into the non-invertible symmetry of the Hopf algebra-valued qudit system in detail and develop the stabilizer formalism for the Hopf cluster state. Additionally, we construct the Hamiltonian for the
1
-dimensional cluster graph.
(iv) We also provide a generalization of cluster states to arbitrary graph and hypergraph based on the pairing of (weak) Hopf algebras, this is more similar to the original definition of qubit graph state in Eq. (
1
).

The paper is organized as follows.
In Sec.
2
, we provide an overview of the fundamental definition of Hopf algebra, followed by a discussion on constructing a Hopf algebra valued qudit and presenting the corresponding generalized Pauli operators.
We also demonstrate that the Hopf qudit naturally embodies non-invertible symmetries.
Sec.
3
is devoted to constructing the CSS-type Hopf cluster state.
Sec.
4
explains how to construct a Hopf cluster state on arbitrary given spatial manifold such that the corresponding entangler circuit is of finite depth.
The Hopf tensor network is introduced in Sec.
5
.
In Sec.
6
, we take one dimensional as an example to illustrate our construction and present the explicit Hamiltonian in this case. We show that the Hopf cluster state model has non-invertible symmetries. In Sec.
7
, the equivalence between the 1d Hopf cluster state model and the quasi-1d Hopf quantum double model is established. In Sec.
8
, we generalize the cluster state model to Hopf ladder model based on symmetry topological field theory (SymTFT).
Sec.
9
is devoted to the construction of the general graph and hypergraph state based on a Hopf algebra.
In the last part, we provide some concluding remarks.

2
Hopf algebra valued qudit

In this section, we introduce the definition of a Hopf algebra and discuss its properties that will be utilized in our construction of Hopf cluster states. We delve into the concept of a Hopf algebra valued qudit, which is a generalization of the finite group valued qudit, and discuss its details. Furthermore, we provide a discussion of the concept of Hopf symmetry and its representation category symmetry for qudits. These concepts play a crucial role in the study of SPT phases for Hopf cluster states, which will be discussed elsewhere
Jia ()
.

2.1
Preliminaries of Hopf algebra theory

Let’s begin by providing an abstract definition of a Hopf algebra before delving into its detailed explanation. For a more comprehensive discussion of Hopf algebras, refer to, e.g., Refs.
Abe (2004)
;
Kassel (1995)
. Our conventions and notations are the same as those used in our previous work on Hopf and weak Hopf quantum double models
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
. As we will see, our construction of Hopf cluster states is also closely related to the Hopf quantum double model, which will be illustrated later in Sec.
7
.

Definition 1
.

A Hopf algebra is a complex vector space
{\cal A}
equipped with several structure linear morphisms: multiplication
\mu:{\cal A}\otimes{\cal A}\to{\cal A}
, unit
\eta:\mathbb{C}\to{\cal A}
, comultiplication
\Delta:{\cal A}\to{\cal A}\otimes{\cal A}
, counit
\varepsilon:{\cal A}\to\mathbb{C}
and antipode
S:{\cal A}\to{\cal A}
, for which some consistency conditions are satisfied:

1.

({\cal A},\mu,\eta)
is an algebra:
\mu\circ(\mu\otimes\operatorname{id})=\mu\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\mu)
, and
\mu\circ(\eta\otimes\operatorname{id})=\operatorname{id}=\mu\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta)
.

2.

({\cal A},\Delta,\varepsilon)
is a coalgebra:
(\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta=(\operatorname{id}\otimes\Delta)\circ\Delta
, and

(\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta=\operatorname{id}=(\operatorname{id}\otimes\varepsilon)\circ\Delta
.

3.

({\cal A},\mu,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon)
is a bialgebra:
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
are algebra homomorphisms (equivalently
\mu
and
\eta
are coalgebra homomorphisms).

4.

The antipode
S
satisfies:
\mu\circ(S\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta=\eta\circ\varepsilon=\mu\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes S)\circ\Delta
.

To accommodate readers with a background in physics, here we provide a detailed discussion of these algebraic concepts.
An algebra structure
(\mathcal{A},\mu,\eta)
can also be represented using string diagrams
Turaev (2016)
;
Bakalov and Kirillov (2001)
. These string diagrams are quite inspiring for our construction of Hopf tensor networks, and they make tensor calculations very intuitive (see Sec.
5
).

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope

(5)

We denote the unit element of
{\cal A}
as
1_{{\cal A}}=\eta(1)
.
The algebra axioms can be represented as

(6)

The group algebra
\mathbb{C}[G]
is a typical example of algebras, for which the multiplication of
x=\sum_{g\in G}x_{g}g
and
y=\sum_{k\in G}y_{k}k
is given by
xy=\sum_{l\in G}(\sum_{g\in G}x_{g}y_{g^{-1}l})l
which is induced by group multiplication.

The coalgebra is particularly crucial, as it outputs a multipartite (entangled) state for a single qudit input. The structure of entanglement is naturally encoded in the comultiplication maps.
Fix a basis
\{g_{i}\}
of
{\cal A}
,
\Delta:{\cal A}\to{\cal A}\otimes{\cal A}
can be represented as

\Delta(x)=\sum_{i,j}X_{ij}g_{i}\otimes g_{j},\quad x\in{\cal A}.

(7)

This can be reformulated as

\Delta(x)=\sum_{i}g_{i}\otimes h_{i},\quad h_{i}=\sum_{j}X_{ij}g_{j},

(8)

which is similar to the Schmidt decomposition of an entangled state.
This type of representation of the coalgebra is known as Sweedler’s notation. Through coassociativity, we can define
\Delta_{n}
recursively via
\Delta_{n}=(\Delta\otimes\text{id})\circ\Delta_{n-1}
without ambiguity. Then, we have the following notation

\Delta_{n}(x)=\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes x^{(n)}.

(9)

See Refs.
Abe (2004)
;
Kassel (1995)
for details. Sweedler’s notation, much like the Einstein summation convention, can simplify calculations involving many variables. We will also omit the sum symbol whenever necessary for avoiding the clustering of equations. It also has a very natural explanation via tensor networks, as will be discussed in Sec.
5
.
This string diagram for coalgebra is dual to that of algebra (by rotating
180^{\circ}
),

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned},\quad\varepsilon=\begin{aligned} \hbox to4.4pt{\vbox to24.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.2pt\lower 20.83957pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

(11)

Notice that expressed in elements, the second equation is of the form
\sum_{(x)}\varepsilon(x^{(1)})x^{(2)}=x=\sum_{(x)}\varepsilon(x^{(2)})x^{(1)}
.
The set
\mathbb{C}[G]
also has a coalgebra structure
\Delta(g)=g\otimes g
and
\varepsilon(g)=1
for all
g\in G
.

(13)

The group algebra
\mathbb{C}[G]
is also a bialgebra.

The antipode map plays a similar role to taking inverses in a group, but in algebra, elements generally do not have inverses. Thus, we use a map
S:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
to characterize this operation.

(14)

Expressed in elements, we have

\sum_{(x)}S(x^{(1)})x^{(2)}=\varepsilon(x)1_{{\cal A}}=\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}S(x^{(2)})
.
The antipode map also satisfies

\displaystyle S(1_{{\cal A}})=1_{{\cal A}},S(xy)=S(y)S(x),\varepsilon(S(x))=\varepsilon(x),

(15)

\displaystyle\sum_{(x)}S(x^{(2)})\otimes S(x^{(1)})=\sum_{(S(x)}S(x)^{(1)}\otimes S(x)^{(2)}.

We will also assume the Hopf algebra to be both semisimple and cosemisimple, implying
S^{2}=\operatorname{id}_{{\cal A}}
by the Larson-Radford theorem
Larson and Radford (1988)
. From
S^{2}=\operatorname{id}_{{\cal A}}
, we obtain

\sum_{(x)}S(x^{(2)})x^{(1)}=\varepsilon(x),

(16)

which plays a crucial role later in constructing entangler gates (See Sec.
3
).

A
*
-operation on a Hopf algbra
{\cal A}
is a antilinear map
*:{\cal A}\to{\cal A}
which satisfies

\displaystyle(x^{*})^{*}=x,(xy)^{*}=y^{*}x^{*},1_{{\cal A}}^{*}=1_{{\cal A}},

(17)

\displaystyle\Delta(x^{*})=(\Delta(x))^{*}.

And
{\cal A}
is called
C^{*}
Hopf algebra if there is a
*
-embedding of
{\cal A}
into the algebra of operators for some Hilbert space.
Hereinafter, we will assume that our Hopf algebra is a
C^{*}
Hopf algebra unless otherwise stated.
For
C^{*}
Hopf algebra, we have

S\circ*\circ S\circ*=\operatorname{id}.

(18)

Using
S^{2}=\operatorname{id}
, we obtain

S(x)^{*}=S(x^{*}).

(19)

Another notion we will use is the Haar integral
\lambda\in{\cal A}
, which is defined as a normalized two-side integral.
A left (resp. right) integral of
{\cal A}
is an element
\ell
(resp.
r
) satisfying
x\ell=\varepsilon(x)\ell
(resp.
rx=r\varepsilon(x)
) for all
x\in{\cal A}
.
If
h
is simultaneously left and right integral, it’s called a two-side integral.
A left (resp. right) integral
\ell
(resp.
r
) is called normalized if
\varepsilon(\ell)=1
(resp.
\varepsilon(r)=1
). We see that normalized integral is idempotent
\ell^{2}=\varepsilon(\ell)\ell=\ell
(resp.
r^{2}=r\varepsilon(r)=r
).
For a semisimple Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
, there exist a unique Haar integral.
When
{\cal A}=\mathbb{C}[G]
, the Haar integral is of the form

\lambda=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}g.

(20)

For general Hopf algebra, the Haar integral can be expressed as in Eq. (
32
).

For a
C^{*}
Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
, the dual space
\bar{{\cal A}}:=\operatorname{Hom}({\cal A},\mathbb{C})
consists of all bounded linear functions from
{\cal A}
to
\mathbb{C}
is also a
C^{*}
Hopf algebra.
Consider the canonical pairing
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle:\bar{{\cal A}}\times{\cal A}\to\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\mu}(\varphi\otimes\psi),x\rangle=\langle\varphi\otimes\psi,\Delta(x)\rangle,

(21)

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\eta}(1),x\rangle=\varepsilon(x),\;\text{i.e.,}\;\bar{1}=\varepsilon,

(22)

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\Delta}(\varphi),x\otimes y\rangle=\langle\varphi,\mu(x\otimes y)\rangle,

(23)

\displaystyle\bar{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=\langle\varphi,\eta(1)\rangle,

(24)

\displaystyle\langle\bar{S}(\varphi),x\rangle=\langle\varphi,S(x)\rangle.

(25)

The
*
-operation on
\bar{{\cal A}}
can be defined as

\langle\varphi^{*},x\rangle=\overline{\langle\varphi,S(x)^{*}\rangle}.

(26)

We denote the Haar integral of
\bar{{\cal A}}
as
\Lambda
, which is also called a Haar measure of
{\cal A}
. For a Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
endowed with a
*
-structure, we can introduce an inner product over
{\cal A}
as

\langle x,y\rangle=\Lambda(x^{*}y).

(27)

It can be proven that the above definition is a complex inner product.
Also notice that for
{\cal A}=\mathbb{C}[G]
, the Haar measure is
\Lambda=\delta_{1_{G}}(\cdot)
.

2.2
Hopf algebra valued qudit and non-invertible Hopf symmetries

With the above preparation, we now introduce the Hopf algebra valued qudit (Hopf qudit), which is, by definition, a
C^{*}
Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
equipped with the inner product in Eq. (
27
).
The standard basis of
{\cal A}
is a basis which includes
1_{{\cal A}}
as a special element:

\{1_{{\cal A}}=g_{0},g_{1},\cdots,g_{d-1}\}.

(28)

Notice that any qudit can be regarded as a Hopf qudit. For example, qudit
\mathbb{C}^{d}
can be regarded as a
d
-dimensional Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{d}]
. The Hopf algebra structure for a given qudit is not unique, and for any
d
, such Hopf algebra structure exists.
Any element
h\in{\cal A}
can be regarded as a state

|h\rangle=\sum_{i=0}^{|{\cal A}|-1}c_{i}|g_{i}\rangle.

(29)

For a
C^{*}
Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
, a representation is an algebra homomorphism
\Gamma:{\cal A}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
, viz.,

\Gamma(g)\Gamma(h)=\Gamma(gh),\quad\Gamma(1_{{\cal A}})=I_{d_{\Gamma}},

(30)

where
d_{\Gamma}=\dim V
is called the dimension of the representation.
In this work, we restrict our attention to finite-dimensional representations. The isomorphism classes of irreps of
{\cal A}
will be denoted as
\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})
.
If we use
\chi_{\Gamma}
to denote the character corresponding to irreducible representation
\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})
, the Haar integral of
\bar{{\cal A}}
can be expressed as
Larson (1971)
:

\Lambda=\frac{1}{|{\cal A}|}\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}d_{\Gamma}\chi_{\Gamma}=\frac{1}{|{\cal A}|}\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\chi_{\Gamma}(1_{{\cal A}})\chi_{\Gamma}.

(31)

From this, we see that the Haar integral of
{\cal A}
can be expressed as

\lambda=\frac{1}{|{\cal A}|}\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}(\bar{{\cal A}})}d_{\Gamma}\chi_{\Gamma}=\frac{1}{|{\cal A}|}\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}(\bar{{\cal A}})}{\varepsilon}(\chi_{\Gamma})\chi_{\Gamma},

(32)

where we have used the fact
\chi_{\Gamma}\in\bar{\bar{{\cal A}}}\cong{\cal A}
is an element in
{\cal A}
.

The trivial representation of
{\cal A}
is
\mathbb{C}
which is equipped with the action
g\triangleright z=\varepsilon(g)z,g\in{\cal A},z\in\mathbb{C}
.
Recall that
\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{C}
, and
\varepsilon:{\cal A}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfies
\varepsilon(gh)=\varepsilon(g)\varepsilon(h)
and
\varepsilon(1_{{\cal A}})=1
.
Unlike the group case, the tensor product of two representations
(\Gamma_{1},V)
and
(\Gamma_{2},W)
is a representation with action defined by

h\triangleright(v\otimes w)=\sum_{(h)}\Gamma_{1}(h^{(1)})v\otimes\Gamma_{2}(h^{(2)})w.

(33)

The dual representation
\bar{\Gamma}
of
(\Gamma,V)
is defined as
\bar{V}:=\operatorname{Hom}(V,\mathbb{C})
, with the left
{\cal A}
action given by
(x\triangleright f)(y)=f(S(x)\triangleright y)
for
f\in\bar{V}
. The category of finite dimensional representations of a Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
will be denoted as
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
.
The categories
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
of finite dimensional representations of
{\cal A}
is a unitary fusion category
Etingof et al. (2016)
.
The fusion rule can be expressed as

\Gamma\otimes\Phi=\oplus_{\Psi}N_{\Gamma,\Phi}^{\Psi}\Psi,

(34)

where
N_{\Gamma,\Phi}^{\Psi}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is called the fusion symbol.
The Grothendieck ring
\operatorname{Gr}(\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A}))
of
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
is
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
-ring defined by

[\Gamma]\cdot[\Phi]=\sum_{\Psi}N_{\Gamma,\Phi}^{\Psi}[\Psi],

(35)

where we have used the notation
[\Gamma]
to stress the equivalence class of irreps.
The Grothendieck ring can also be expressed using the characters of irreps, we define

\chi_{\Gamma}\cdot\chi_{\Phi}:=\chi_{\Gamma\otimes\Phi}=\sum_{\Psi}N_{\Gamma,\Phi}^{\Psi}\chi_{\Psi}.

(36)

Notice that
\chi_{\Gamma}
’s are functions over
{\cal A}
, thus
\chi_{\Gamma}\in\bar{{\cal A}}
. It satisfies

\chi_{\Gamma}(ab)=\chi_{\Gamma}(ba),

(37)

which implies that
\chi_{\Gamma}
is cocommutative (from Eq. (
23
)).
The multiplication in Eq. (
36
) is also consistent with that of Eq. (
21
):

\langle\chi_{\Gamma}\cdot\chi_{\Phi},x\rangle=\sum_{(x)}\chi_{\Gamma}(x^{(1)})\chi_{\Phi}(x^{(2)})=\sum_{\Psi}N_{\Gamma,\Phi}^{\Psi}\chi_{\Psi}(x).

(38)

The above equations will be crucial for establishing the Hopf cluster state model and the Hopf quantum double model.

The standard basis plays a similar role as Pauli-Z basis
|0\rangle,|1\rangle
for a qubit.
For
\Gamma\in\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
, we introduce generalized Pauli-Z operators as follows:

Z_{\Gamma}|h\rangle=\sum_{(h)}|h^{(1)}\rangle\otimes\Gamma(h^{(2)}),

(39)

which is well-defined since
Z_{\Gamma}=(\text{id}\otimes\Gamma)\circ\Delta
. Recall that
\Gamma:\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
has its domain in
\mathcal{A}
.
Similarly, we define

Z^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}|h\rangle=\sum_{(h)}\Gamma(S(h^{(1)}))\otimes|h^{(2)}\rangle.

(40)

We can also introduce

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}|h\rangle=\sum_{(h)}\Gamma(h^{(1)})\otimes|h^{(2)}\rangle,

(41)

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}|h\rangle=\sum_{(h)}|h^{(1)}\rangle\otimes\Gamma(S(h^{(2)})).

(42)

When
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{C}[G]
,
Z_{\Gamma}
and
Z^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}
take the same forms as Eqs (11) and (12) in Ref.
Fechisin et al. (2023)
. And in this case, we have
Z_{\Gamma}=\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}
and
Z_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}=\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}
(up to a reordering), since
\Delta(g)=g\otimes g
for all
g\in G
.
A similar notation is
Z_{\Gamma_{ij}}=(\text{id}\otimes\Gamma_{ij})\circ\Delta
:

Z_{\Gamma_{ij}}|h\rangle=\sum_{(h)}\Gamma_{ij}(h^{(2)})|h^{(1)}\rangle.

(43)

We can also introduce
Z_{\Gamma_{ij}}^{\ddagger}
,
\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma_{ij}}
and
\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma_{ij}}^{\ddagger}
in a similar way.
The comultiplication plays a crucial role, as we will see later, we cannot use a naive generalization of group case via
\sum_{k}\Gamma(g_{k})\otimes|g_{k}\rangle\langle g_{k}|
.

The trace in the representation space
{V}
will be denoted as
\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}
. Then, it’s clear that

(\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z_{\Gamma})|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=d_{\Gamma}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle.

(44)

We introduce

Z=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}(\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z_{\Gamma}),

(45)

and we have

Z|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle.

(46)

The unit element state plays the same role as that of Pauli-Z
+1
eigenstate
|0\rangle
,
Z|0\rangle=|0\rangle
.

We introduce the following operators by tracing over representation spaces

J_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z_{\Gamma},J^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma},\tilde{J}_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma},\tilde{J}^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\tilde{Z}^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}.

(47)

Recall that in the quantum double model, we defined the left action of
\bar{{\cal A}}
on
{\cal A}
as

T^{\varphi}_{+}|x\rangle=\sum_{(x)}\varphi(x^{(2)})|x^{(1)}\rangle,\quad T^{\varphi}_{-}|x\rangle=\sum_{(x)}\bar{S}(\varphi)(x^{(1)})|x^{(2)}\rangle.

(48)

The right action of
\bar{{\cal A}}
on
{\cal A}
are

\tilde{T}^{\varphi}_{+}|x\rangle=\sum_{(x)}\bar{S}(\varphi)(x^{(2)})|x^{(1)}\rangle,\quad\tilde{T}^{\varphi}_{-}|x\rangle=\sum_{(x)}\varphi(x^{(1)})|x^{(2)}\rangle.

(49)

We have adopted the conventions we used in Ref.
(
Jia et al., 2023b
, Eqs. (4.1)-(4.8))
for quantum double model.
It’s easy to verify that
J_{\Gamma}
is the same as
T^{\chi_{\Gamma}}_{+}
. In fact, we have the following correspondence:

\displaystyle J_{\Gamma}=T^{\chi_{\Gamma}}_{+},\quad J_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}=T^{\chi_{\Gamma}}_{-},

(50)

\displaystyle\tilde{J}_{\Gamma}=\tilde{T}^{\chi_{\Gamma}}_{-},\quad\tilde{J}^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}=\tilde{T}^{\chi_{\Gamma}}_{+}.

(51)

Eq. (
31
) and the fact that Haar measure
\Lambda
in invariant under the action of antipode implies that

T^{\Lambda}_{+}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}(\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z_{\Gamma})=Z=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}(\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\tilde{Z}^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma})=\tilde{T}_{+}^{\Lambda},

(52)

\tilde{T}^{\Lambda}_{-}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}(\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma})=\tilde{Z}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}(\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}{Z}^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma})={T}^{\Lambda}_{-}.

(53)

In fact, for Haar measure
\Lambda
, we have

\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}\Lambda(x^{(2)})=\Lambda(x)=\sum_{(x)}x^{(2)}\Lambda(x^{(1)}).

(54)

This can be derived by taking
h=1
for Eqs. (16) and (17) in Ref.
Nikshych (2002)
.
Combining Eq. (
54
) and Eqs. (
52
) and (
53
), we obtain

Z=\tilde{Z},\quad Z|x\rangle=\Lambda(x)|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle\langle 1_{{\cal A}}|x\rangle,\forall x\in{\cal A}.

(55)

Notice that
Z^{2}=T_{+}^{\Lambda^{2}}=T_{+}^{\Lambda}
. Since
\Lambda^{2}=\Lambda
, we see that
Z
is idempotent.
From
Z^{\dagger}=(T_{+}^{\Lambda})^{\dagger}=T_{+}^{\Lambda^{*}}
(this is not straightforward; see the proof of Theorem
1
for a discussion), and using
\Lambda^{*}=\Lambda
, we see that
Z
is Hermitian.

These correspondence will play crucial roles in Sec.
7
for establishing the equivalence between 1d Hopf cluster state model and quasi-1d Hopf quantum double model.

Hopf qudit

\mathcal{H}_{v}={\cal A}

Standard basis

|g_{0}\rangle=|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle,\cdots,|g_{|{\cal A}|-1}\rangle

Irrep basis

|\Gamma_{ij}\rangle=\sqrt{{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}}\sum_{(\lambda)}\Gamma_{ij}(\lambda^{(1)})|\lambda^{(2)}\rangle

Regular action

\begin{aligned} &\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g}|h\rangle=|gh\rangle,\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g}|h\rangle=|hS(g)\rangle\\
&\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{g}|h\rangle=|S(g)h\rangle,\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{g}|h\rangle=|hg\rangle\end{aligned}

Irrep action

\begin{aligned} &Z_{\Gamma}|h\rangle=\sum_{(h)}|h^{(1)}\rangle\otimes\Gamma(h^{(2)})\\
&Z^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}|h\rangle=\sum_{(h)}\Gamma(S(h^{(1)}))\otimes|h^{(2)}\rangle\\
&\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}=\sum_{(h)}\Gamma(h^{(1)})\otimes|h^{(2)}\rangle\\
&\tilde{Z}^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}=\sum_{(h)}|h^{(1)}\rangle\otimes\Gamma(S(h^{(2)}))\end{aligned}

Symmetry action

\begin{aligned} &J_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z_{\Gamma},J^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}\\
&\tilde{J}_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z_{\Gamma},\tilde{J}^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}\end{aligned}

Generalized Pauli operators

\begin{aligned} &X=\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{\lambda}=\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{\lambda}=\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{\lambda}=\tilde{X}\\
&Z=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}J_{\Gamma}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}\tilde{J}_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}\\
&\tilde{Z}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}\tilde{J}_{\Gamma}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}{J}_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}\\
&Z=\tilde{Z}\end{aligned}

Table 1:
Summary of the quantum operations for Hopf qudit system.

We have constructed the generalized Pauli-Z operators and the standard basis, which play the role of qubit
Z
operator and
|0\rangle,|1\rangle
states. Let’s now construct the generalized Pauli-X operator and the states corresponding to
|+\rangle,|-\rangle
states.
Before that, we will first prove some results about the irreps of Hopf algebras. While these results may be familiar to experts in Hopf algebra, they do not appear elsewhere in the physical literature. Therefore, for comprehensiveness and convenience, we provide the proofs here.

Proposition 1

(Schur’s lemma for (weak) Hopf algebra)
.

Let
\Gamma:{\cal A}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
and
\Phi:{\cal A}\to\operatorname{End}(W)
be two irreducible representations of
{\cal A}
, and let
f:V\to W
be a linear map such that
\Phi(x)\circ f=f\circ\Gamma(x)
for all
x\in{\cal A}
. Then (i) If
\Gamma
and
\Phi
are not isomorphic,
f=0
; (ii) When
\Gamma=\Phi
, we have
f\propto\operatorname{id}_{V}
.

Proof.

(i) Consider the nontrivial case that
f\neq 0
. We see that
\operatorname{Ker}f
is stable under the action of
{\cal A}
. Since for
v\in\operatorname{Ker}f
,
\Phi(x)\circ f(v)=f(\Gamma(x)v)=0
. Thus
\Gamma(x)v\in\operatorname{Ker}f
for all
x\in{\cal A}
. Since
(\Gamma,V)
is irreducible,
\operatorname{Ker}f
can only be
0
or
V
.
A similar argument shows that
\operatorname{Im}f=W
. Thus
f
is an isomorphism.

(ii) Since
f
is a linear map over a complex field, thus must be at least one eigenvalue
p
for
f
. Then
\operatorname{Ker}(f-p\operatorname{id})\neq 0
. This implies that
f-p\operatorname{id}=1
from assertion (i).
∎

For Haar integral
\lambda\in{\cal A}
and arbitrary
x\in{\cal A}
, we have the following
(
Bridgeman et al., 2023
, Lemma 2)

\sum_{(\lambda)}xS(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}=S(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}x.

(56)

Then for a linear map
f:V\to W
, we define

F=\sum_{(\lambda)}\Phi(S(\lambda^{(1)}))\circ f\circ\Gamma(\lambda^{(2)}).

(57)

It’s clear from Eq. (
56
) that

\Phi(x)\circ F=F\circ\Gamma(x),\quad\forall x\in{\cal A}.

(58)

Proposition 2
.

(1) For any
f:V\to W
, and define
F
as in Eq. (
57
), we have: (i) If
\Gamma
and
\Phi
are not isomorphic,
F=0
; (ii) when
\Gamma=\Phi
, we have

F=\frac{1}{d_{\Gamma}}\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}(f)\operatorname{id}_{V}.

(59)

(2) From the above result, we obtain the orthogonality relation for irreps of
{\cal A}
:

\sum_{(\lambda)}\Phi_{ij}(S(\lambda^{(1)}))\Gamma_{kl}(\lambda^{(2)})=\frac{\delta_{\Phi,\Gamma}\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}}{d_{\Gamma}}=\sum_{(\lambda)}\Phi_{ij}(S(\lambda^{(2)}))\Gamma_{kl}(\lambda^{(1)}).

(60)

where we have used the cocommutativity of
\lambda
.

Proof.

(1) This is a direct result of Proposition
1
. Notice that

\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}F=\sum_{(\lambda)}\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}[\Gamma(S(\lambda^{(1)}))\circ f\circ\Gamma(\lambda^{(2)})]=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}[\Gamma(\varepsilon(\lambda)1_{{\cal A}})f]=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}f,

(61)

which implies Eq. (
59
).

(2) From (1), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda)}\sum_{jk}\Phi_{ij}(S(\lambda^{(1)}))f_{jk}\Gamma_{kl}(\lambda^{(2)})=\frac{\delta_{\Phi,\Gamma}}{d_{\Gamma}}\delta_{il}\sum_{jk}f_{jk}\delta_{jk}.

(62)

This implies the required result.
∎

For irreps
\Gamma\in\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
, the fusion basis states are defined using Haar integral
\lambda
as follows:

|\Gamma_{ij}\rangle=\sqrt{{d_{\Gamma}|{\cal A}|}}\sum_{(\lambda)}\Gamma_{ij}(\lambda^{(1)})|\lambda^{(2)}\rangle,

(63)

where
i,j=1,\cdots,d_{\Gamma}
. From Peter-Weyl relation
H\cong\oplus_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\bar{\Gamma}\otimes\Gamma
we see that
|{\cal A}|=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}d_{\Gamma}^{2}
.
Also notice that
\lambda
is cocommutative
\sum_{(\lambda)}\lambda^{(1)}\otimes\lambda^{(2)}=\sum_{(\lambda)}\lambda^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(1)}
, we can equivalently define

|\Gamma_{ij}\rangle=\sqrt{{d_{\Gamma}|{\cal A}|}}\sum_{(\lambda)}\Gamma_{ij}(\lambda^{(2)})|\lambda^{(1)}\rangle,

(64)

Also note that the factor chosen here is different from that in Refs.
Buerschaper et al. (2013b)
;
Yan et al. (2022)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
. This adjustment ensures that the basis is orthonormal under the inner product in Eq. (
27
).

Proposition 3
.

The fusion basis
|\Gamma_{ij}\rangle
is an orthonormal basis for Hopf qudit
{\cal A}
.

Proof.

For clarity, we use
\eta,\lambda
to denote the unique Haar integral of
{\cal A}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{ij}|\Gamma_{kl}\rangle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda),(\eta)}|{\cal A}|\sqrt{d_{\Gamma}d_{\Phi}}\Phi^{*}_{ij}(\eta^{(1)})\Gamma(\lambda^{(1)})\Lambda((\eta^{(2)})^{*}\lambda^{(2)})

(65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda),(\eta)}|{\cal A}|\sqrt{d_{\Gamma}d_{\Phi}}\Phi^{\dagger}_{ji}(\eta^{(1)})\Gamma(\lambda^{(1)})\Lambda((\eta^{(2)})^{*}\lambda^{(2)}),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda),(\eta)}|{\cal A}|\sqrt{d_{\Gamma}d_{\Phi}}\Phi_{ji}((\eta^{(1)})^{*})\Gamma(\lambda^{(1)})\Lambda((\eta^{(2)})^{*}\lambda^{(2)}),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda),(\eta)}|{\cal A}|\sqrt{d_{\Gamma}d_{\Phi}}\Phi_{ji}((\eta^{(1)}))\Gamma(\lambda^{(1)})\Lambda(\eta^{(2)}\lambda^{(2)}),

where
\Lambda
is Haar measure, we have used the property of unitary representation
\Gamma^{\dagger}(x)=\Gamma(x^{*})
and the properties for Haar integral
\eta=\eta^{*}
and for
*
-operation
\Delta(\lambda^{*})=\Delta(\lambda)^{*}
.
Using the property of Haar measure (see Ref.
(
Nikshych, 2002
, Eq. (16))
)

\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}\Lambda(yx^{(2)})=\sum_{(y)}S(y^{(1)})\Lambda(y^{(2)}x),

(66)

and cocommutativity of
\Lambda
, we obtain (ommiting the summation symbol for comultipication)

\displaystyle\eta^{(1)}\Lambda(\eta^{(2)}\lambda^{(2)})=

\displaystyle\eta^{(1)}\Lambda(\lambda^{(2)}\eta^{(2)})=S(\lambda^{(2)})\Lambda(\eta\lambda^{(3)})

(67)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\lambda^{(3)})S(\lambda^{(2)})\Lambda(\eta)=S(\lambda^{(2)})\frac{1}{|{\cal A}|}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{ij}|\Gamma_{kl}\rangle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda),(\eta)}d_{\Gamma}d_{\Phi}\Phi_{ji}(\eta^{(1)})\Gamma(\lambda^{(1)})\Lambda((\eta^{(2)})\lambda^{(2)})

(68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda)}|{\cal A}|\sqrt{d_{\Gamma}d_{\Phi}}\Phi_{ji}(S(\lambda^{(2)}))\Gamma_{kl}(\lambda^{(1)})\frac{1}{|{\cal A}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{d_{\Gamma}d_{\Phi}}\frac{\delta_{\Phi,\Gamma}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}}{d_{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\Phi,\Gamma}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl},

where we have used the orthogonality relation in Proposition
2
.
∎

If we choose
\Gamma
as trivial representation
\Gamma=\mathbb{1}
, then
d_{\Gamma}=1
and
\Gamma(g)=\varepsilon(g)
, this implies that

|\mathbb{1}\rangle={\sqrt{|{\cal A}|}}|\lambda\rangle.

(69)

We introduce the following generalized Pauli-X operators

\displaystyle\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g}:|h\rangle\mapsto|gh\rangle,\quad\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g}:|h\rangle\mapsto|hS(g)\rangle.

(70)

They are left and right regular actions of
{\cal A}
on itself. We can also introduce the right regular action
\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{g}
and
\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{g}
in a similar fashion:

\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{g}:|h\rangle\to|S(g)h\rangle,\quad\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{g}:|h\rangle\to|hg\rangle.

(71)

Recall that in Refs.
Kitaev (2003)
;
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Yan et al. (2022)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
, these operators are denoted as
L_{\pm}^{g},\tilde{L}_{\pm}^{g}
.
From the definition of Haar integral and
S(\lambda)=\lambda
, it’s clear that

\displaystyle\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g}|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\varepsilon(g)|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g}|\mathbb{1}\rangle,\quad\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{g}|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\varepsilon(g)|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{g}|\mathbb{1}\rangle,

(72)

If we set
X=\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{\lambda}
(notice that
\lambda=S(\lambda)
, from which it’s easy to show
\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{\lambda}
), we have

X|\mathbb{1}\rangle=|\mathbb{1}\rangle.

(73)

Similarly, we can set
\tilde{X}=\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{\lambda}=\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{\lambda}
, and it’s clear
X=\tilde{X}
.
From
\lambda^{*}=\lambda
and
\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}=\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda^{*}}
, we see that
X
is Hermitian. From
\lambda^{2}=\lambda
, we see that
X
is a projector.
The trivial representations state plays the same role as that of Pauli-X
+1
eigenstate
|+\rangle
,
X|+\rangle=|+\rangle
.

2.3
Non-invertible symmetries: Hopf symmetry and fusion categorical symmetry

For a quantum system governed by the Hamiltonian operator
H:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
, it exhibits a (group)
G
-symmetry if the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
functions as a
\mathbb{C}[G]
-module, and the representations
U_{g}
satisfy
[U_{g},H]=0
for all
g\in G
. From the definition of representation, we see that all
U_{g}
must be invertible and
U_{g}^{-1}=U_{g^{-1}}
. In this context, ‘invertible symmetry’ refers to the group symmetry.
The representation is termed unitary when
U_{g}^{\dagger}=U_{g}^{-1}
. It’s worth noting that all finite group symmetries are equivalent to unitary symmetries. However, for continuous groups, while they are invertible, they are not necessarily unitary in general.
If the ground state space of
H
is non-degenerate, the unitarity of
U_{g}
implies
U_{g}|\psi_{\rm GS}\rangle=|\psi_{\rm GS}\rangle
.
Therefore, for elements in group algebra
x\in\mathbb{C}[G]
,
[U_{x},H]=0
and
U_{x}|\psi_{\rm GS}\rangle=(\sum_{g\in G}x_{g}U_{g})|\psi_{\rm GS}\rangle=\varepsilon(x)|\psi_{\rm GS}\rangle
.

Non-invertible symmetry is defined as a symmetry characterized by an algebraic structure (e.g., ring, Hopf algebra, comodule algebra, category) where
U_{g}
is not generally invertible
Cordova et al. (2022)
;
Brennan and Hong (2023)
;
McGreevy (2023)
;
Luo et al. (2024)
;
Shao (2024)
;
Schäfer-Nameki (2024)
;
Bhardwaj et al. (2024a)
.
Hopf symmetry is a typical example of non-invertible symmetry
Bais et al. (2003)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
;
Jia et al. (2024a)
. A thorough discussion of Hopf symmetry for Hopf cluster states and its SPT phases will be provided elsewhere
Jia ()
.
See Sec.
3.3
and Sec.
6
for brief discussions.
In this part, we will only provide a discussion from the perspective of a single Hopf qudit.
For a Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}
, a quantum system that exhibits a
\mathcal{A}
Hopf symmetry must form a representation
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{A}
, denoted by
\Gamma:\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{V})
.
It’s notable that
\Phi(h)
is typically non-invertible. Unlike group algebra, it’s often impossible to identify a set of invertible basis elements for a general Hopf algebra.
However, we can still define the symmetry as a representation
U_{x}
such that
[U_{x},H]=0
for all
x\in{\cal A}
.
This non-invertibility implies that
U_{x}|\psi_{\rm GS}\rangle\neq|\psi_{\rm GS}\rangle
generally.
A state
|\psi\rangle\in\mathcal{V}
is called Hopf invariant if
Bais et al. (2003)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)

\Phi(h)|\psi\rangle=\varepsilon(h)|\psi\rangle,\forall h\in{\cal A}.

(74)

For Hopf qudit, if we define the Hamiltonian as

H_{X}=-X=-\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda}=-\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{\lambda}=-\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{\lambda}=-\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{\lambda}.

(75)

It’s clear that
[\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g},H]=0
for all
g\in{\cal A}
, which means that
H
has a non-invertible
{\cal A}
-symmetry. Similarly, we have
{\cal A}
-symmetry
\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g}
, and two
{\cal A}^{\rm op}
-symmetries:
\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{g}
and
\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{g}
, where
{\cal A}^{\rm op}
means
g\cdot^{\rm op}h:=h\cdot g
.

Proposition 4
.

The Hopf qudit Hamiltonian
H_{X}
has two Hopf
{\cal A}
-symmetries
\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g},\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g}
if we treat
H_{X}=-\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda}=-\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{\lambda}
, and it has two
{\cal A}^{op}
-symmetries
\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{g},\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{g}
if we treat
H_{X}=-\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{\lambda}=-\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{\lambda}
.
The trivial representation state
|\mathbb{1}\rangle
is a ground state of Hamiltonian in Eq. (
75
), and we have:

(i) The trivial representation state
|\mathbb{1}\rangle
is Hopf invariant under Hopf
{\cal A}
-symmetry
\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{h}
,
\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{h}
:

\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{h}|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\varepsilon(h)|\mathbb{1}\rangle,\quad\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{h}|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\varepsilon(h)|\mathbb{1}\rangle.

(76)

(ii) The state
|\mathbb{1}\rangle
is also Hopf invariant under Hopf
{\cal A}^{\rm op}
-symmetry
\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{h}
,
\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{h}
. It’s clear that
\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{g}\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{h}=\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{g\cdot^{\rm op}h}
, and
\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{g}\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{h}=\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{g\cdot^{\rm op}h}
. For state
|\mathbb{1}\rangle
, we have

\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{h}|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\varepsilon(h)|\mathbb{1}\rangle,\quad\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{h}|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\varepsilon(h)|\mathbb{1}\rangle.

(77)

Proof.

This is a direct result of the fact that, for Haar integral
\lambda
,
S(\lambda)=\lambda
and
x\lambda=\lambda x=\varepsilon(x)\lambda
.
∎

As we will see later, the Hopf cluster state has Hopf symmetries (Sec.
3.3
and Sec.
6
).
It’s also worth mentioning that the ground state of the Hopf quantum double model also exhibits this type of Hopf symmetry
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
.
Also note that if we choose
h\in\operatorname{Comm}({\cal A})
as an element in the center of
{\cal A}
and set
H=-\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{h}
, we see that
H
will have Hopf
{\cal A}
-symmetry given by
\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g}
. This indicates that non-invertible Hopf symmetry for Hopf qudit system is a common phenomenon.

Another kind of non-invertible symmetry is the categorical symmetry, which is usually characterized by the unitary fusion category
Fröhlich et al. (2004)
;
Fuchs et al. (2007)
;
Fröhlich et al. (2010)
;
Bhardwaj and Tachikawa (2018)
;
Chang et al. (2019)
;
Thorngren and Wang (2024)
;
Thorngren and Wang (2021)
;
Komargodski et al. (2021)
;
Inamura (2022)
;
Inamura (2023)
.
The Hopf qudit possesses a categorical symmetry described by
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
, for which we have the fusion rule

\Phi\otimes\Gamma=\oplus_{\Psi\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}N_{\Phi\Gamma}^{\Psi}\Psi,

(78)

where
N_{\Phi\Gamma}^{\Psi}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is called fusion multiplicity.
The symmetry operators
U_{\Gamma}
are labeled by objects
\Gamma\in\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
, their composition law is give by the fusion rule

U_{\Gamma}U_{\Phi}=U_{\Gamma\otimes\Phi}.

(79)

We can similarly introduce the
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
symmetry, where
\Gamma\otimes^{\rm rev}\Phi:=\Phi\otimes\Gamma
.

Proposition 5
.

If we define the Hamiltonian for Hopf qudit as

H=-Z=-\tilde{Z}.

(80)

The Hopf qudit have non-invertible
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
and
\mathsf{Rep}^{\rm rev}({\cal A})
symmetries.

(i)
If we treat the Hamiltonian for Hopf qudit as

H_{Z}=-Z=-\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}(\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z_{\Gamma}),.

(81)

The Hopf qudit system will have a non-invertible symmetry characterized by the category
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
.
The symmetry operator is given by
J_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z_{\Gamma}
, and we have

J_{\Gamma}\circ J_{\Phi}=J_{\Gamma\otimes\Phi}=J_{\oplus_{\Psi\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}N_{\Phi\Gamma}^{\Psi}\Psi}.

(82)

For ground state
|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle
,

J_{\Gamma}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=d_{\Gamma}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle.

(83)

If we define
J_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}Z_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}
, we have

J_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}J_{\Phi}^{\ddagger}=J^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma\otimes\Phi}=J^{\ddagger}_{\oplus_{\Psi\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}N_{\Gamma\Phi}^{\Psi}\Psi}.

(84)

This also gives a
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
symmetry and we also have

J_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=d_{\Gamma}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle.

(85)

(ii)
If we treat the Hamiltonian for Hopf qudit as

H_{\tilde{Z}}=-\tilde{Z}=-\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}(\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}),.

(86)

The Hopf qudit system will have a non-invertible symmetry characterized by the category
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
, where
\Gamma\otimes^{\rm rev}\Phi:=\Phi\otimes\Gamma
.
The symmetry is realized by
E_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}
and
E_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\tilde{Z}^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}
.
We have

\displaystyle E_{\Gamma}E_{\Phi}=E_{\Gamma\otimes^{\rm rev}\Phi}=E_{\oplus_{\Psi\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}N_{\Phi\Gamma}^{\Psi}\Psi}

(87)

\displaystyle E_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}E_{\Phi}^{\ddagger}=E_{\Gamma\otimes^{\rm rev}\Phi}^{\ddagger}=E_{\oplus_{\Psi\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}N_{\Phi\Gamma}^{\Psi}\Psi}^{\ddagger},

(88)

and for state
|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle
,

E_{\Gamma}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=d_{\Gamma}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=E_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle.

(89)

Proof.

To prove this, recall that
Z_{\Gamma}=(\text{id}\otimes\Gamma)\circ\Delta
, this means that

J_{\Gamma}=(\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{Tr})\circ(\text{id}\otimes\Gamma)\circ\Delta,

(90)

viz.,
J_{\Gamma}
is map form Hopf qudit
{\cal A}
to itself. Then for
h\in{\cal A}
, we have

\displaystyle J_{\Gamma}\circ J_{\Phi}|h\rangle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(h)}J_{\Gamma}|h^{(1)}\rangle\otimes\operatorname{Tr}\Phi(h^{(2)})

(91)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(h)}|h^{(1)}\rangle\otimes\operatorname{Tr}\Gamma(h^{(2)})\otimes\operatorname{Tr}\Phi(h^{(3)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(h)}|h^{(1)}\otimes\operatorname{Tr}[(\Gamma\otimes\Phi)(h^{(2)})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(h)}|h^{(1)}\otimes\operatorname{Tr}[(\oplus_{\Psi\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}N_{\Phi\Gamma}^{\Psi}\Psi)(h^{(2)})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J_{\oplus_{\Psi\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}N_{\Phi\Gamma}^{\Psi}\Psi}|h\rangle.

Notice that we have used the definition of tensor product for two representations given in Eq. (
33
). A more intuitive proof based Hopf tensor network will be given in Sec.
5
. All other equations can be proven analogously.

We also need to show that
[J_{\Gamma},H_{Z}]=0
. This is clear from the fact that
J_{\Gamma}=T^{\chi_{\Gamma}}_{+}
and
H_{Z}=T^{\Lambda}_{+}
, with
\chi_{\Gamma}
being the character of
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
the Haar measure of
{\cal A}
. See Sec.
7
for a more detailed discussion.

All other claims can be proved in similar ways.
∎

Notice that if we use the characters
\chi_{\Gamma}
to represent the categorical symmetry, we see that the
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
symmetry can be regarded as a subalgebra of the dual Hopf algebra
\bar{{\cal A}}
.
The Hopf qudit
{\cal A}
can be regarded as a left
\bar{{\cal A}}
-module with the left action given by
T^{\varphi}_{+}
, viz., we have

T^{\bar{1}}_{+}=\operatorname{id}_{{\cal A}},\quad T^{\varphi\psi}_{+}=T^{\varphi}_{+}T_{+}^{\psi},

(92)

where
\bar{1}=\varepsilon
is the unit of
\bar{{\cal A}}
. Using Eqs. (
50
) and (
51
), we see that the
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
and
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
symmetries discussed above can be embedded into
\bar{{\cal A}}
.

3
CSS-type Hopf Cluster states

With the preparation outlined above, in this section, we will construct the CSS-type Hopf cluster state, which we will simply refer to as the Hopf cluster state. Our construction has the property that it can recover the finite group cluster state
Brell (2015)
;
Fechisin et al. (2023)
when we set the Hopf algebra as the group algebra.

Indeed, our construction relies on a bipartite graph
K=(V,E)
, where each odd vertex has a local ordering of edges connected to it. We term this type of graph a cluster graph. Below is a formal definition (See Fig.
1
for a depiction):

Definition 2

(Cluster graph)
.

A graph
K(V,E)
is called a cluster graph if (i) it is a bipartite graph; (ii) each edge is oriented; and (iii) there is global ordering of edge set, denoted as
e_{1},\cdots,e_{|E|}
.

Figure 1:
An illustration of cluster graph. The red vertices represent odd vertices, while the teal vertices represent even vertices. The vertices are labeled with the ordering from
1
to
20
, and examples of local ordering for edges connected to odd vertex
v_{7}
are shown as ①,
\cdots
, and ④.

The condition (i) implies that we can partition the vertices into even and odd vertices, denoted as
V_{e}
and
V_{o}
, respectively. Furthermore, there is no edge connecting odd vertices with odd vertices, and similarly for even vertices.
This means that the edge set can be divided into disjoint union
E=\sqcup_{v_{o}\in V_{o}}N_{E}(v_{o})
, with
N_{E}(v_{o})
the set of edges connected to odd vertex
v_{o}
.

The global ordering of the edge set in condition (iii) is equivalent to the existence of local orderings for all vertices. Notice that for the group case, we only need to have a local ordering for the edge set
N_{E}(v_{e})
for all even vertices
Brell (2015)
, since the group algebra is cocommutative.
For the Hopf qudit, the local ordering of
N_{E}(v_{o})
plays a crucial role. To ensure a finite-depth circuit, we can choose a special configuration of edge orientation for
N_{E}(v_{e})
at each even vertex
v_{e}
. This will be illustrated in Sec.
4
.

3.1
Vertex states and edge entangler operations

For a cluster graph
K(V,E)
, we assign Hopf qudit
\mathcal{H}_{v}={\cal A}
to each vertex
v\in V
and the total space is
\otimes_{v\in V}\mathcal{H}_{v}
. This kind of Hopf qudit network also plays a crucial role in the Hopf quantum double model
Kitaev (2003)
;
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Buerschaper et al. (2013b)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
.
The initial state of the cluster state is a product of Pauli-X preferred states and Pauli-Z preferred states. This concept can be generalized to Hopf qudits as follows:

Definition 3
.

We introduce two preferred states in the Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
, one is unit element
|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle
call Z-preferred state; the other is trivial representation state
|\mathbb{1}\rangle
called X-preferred state.

As we have proved, the Z and X preferred states satisfy the following properties:

1.

For all irreps
\Gamma\in\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
, we have
Z_{\Gamma^{ii}}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle
,
J_{\Gamma}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=d_{\Gamma}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle
,
Z|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle
.

2.

X
-preferred state
|\mathbb{1}\rangle
satisfies
\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g}|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\varepsilon(g)|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g}|\mathbb{1}\rangle
and
X|\mathbb{1}\rangle=|\mathbb{1}\rangle
.

The first statement comes from the fact
\Delta(1_{{\cal A}})=1_{{\cal A}}\otimes 1_{{\cal A}}
, while the second is a property of the Haar integral. For a cluster graph
K
, we assign
X
-preferred state
|\mathbb{1}\rangle
to each odd vertex and
X
-preferred state
|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle
to each even vertex:

|\Omega\rangle=(\otimes_{v_{o}}|\mathbb{1}\rangle_{v_{o}})\otimes(\otimes_{v_{e}}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle_{v_{e}}),

(93)

which will be called the preferred product state.

The edge entangler for a finite group cluster state manifests as controlled gates, inherently dependent on the chosen basis. At first glance, it might seem plausible to generalize the edge entangler solely using the algebraic structure of
\mathcal{A}
and a selected basis, without resorting to the coalgebra structure and antipode morphism. However, such an approach is not feasible. The reasoning behind this limitation is as follows.
Choosing a basis
\{1_{{\cal A}}=g_{0},g_{1},\cdots,g_{d-1}\}
of the
d
dimensional Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
, using the regular
{\cal A}
action on
{\cal A}
, we could define

\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g_{k}}:|h\rangle\mapsto|g_{k}h\rangle,\quad k=0,\cdots,d-1.

(94)

However, unlike in the group case, the basis elements of a Hopf algebra are generally not invertible. This noninvertibility poses several challenges.
We cannot define
\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g_{k}}
solely using the algebraic structure of
\mathcal{A}
, as there may not be an inverse
g_{k}^{-1}
. To address this, we must utilize the antipode map
S:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
and define

\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g_{k}}:|h\rangle\mapsto|hS(g_{k})\rangle,\quad k=0,\cdots,d-1.

(95)

An even more significant issue arises when defining the edge entangler operation as

C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}=\sum_{k,l=0}^{d-1}|g_{k}\rangle\langle g_{k}|\otimes|g_{k}g_{l}\rangle\langle g_{l}|,

as this map is generally non-invertible. A similar issue exists for
C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}
.

Drawing from the aforementioned observations, we define the edge entangler by leveraging the comprehensive structure of a Hopf algebra. This includes not only the algebraic structure (as a regular action) but also the coalgebra structure and the antipode map.
For the left action, the edge entangler is defined as follows:

C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{i,j}:|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}\mapsto\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|g^{(1)}h\rangle_{j},

(96)

which is invertible, and its inverse is given by:

C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{i,j}^{-1}:|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}\mapsto\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|S(g^{(1)})h\rangle_{j}.

(97)

Notice that
C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}:{\cal A}\otimes{\cal A}\to{\cal A}\otimes{\cal A}
can be expressed as a composition of structure maps of Hopf algebra

C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}=(\text{id}\otimes\mu)\circ(\tau\otimes\text{id})\circ(\Delta\otimes\text{id}),

(98)

where
\tau(a\otimes b)=b\otimes a
is the twist map. All other entangler operations can be expressed in a similar form as well.
We could also define edge entangler from the right regular action of Hopf algebra on itself as follows:

C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{i,j}:|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}\mapsto\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|hS(g^{(1)})\rangle_{j},

(99)

and its inverse is given by:

C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{i,j}^{-1}:|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}\mapsto\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|hg^{(1)}\rangle_{j}.

(100)

Observe that by setting the Hopf algebra as the group algebra
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{C}[G]
, the aforementioned definitions align seamlessly with finite group case
Brell (2015)
;
Fechisin et al. (2023)
.

To show that
C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{i,j}^{-1}\circ C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{i,j}=\operatorname{id}_{{\cal A}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{{\cal A}}
, we need to use the fact
S^{2}=\operatorname{id}_{{\cal A}}
when
{\cal A}
is semisimple and cosemisimple
Larson and Radford (1988)
, this further implies
S(g^{(2)})g^{(1)}=\varepsilon(g)1_{{\cal A}}
. With this and the Hopf algebra axiom
\sum_{(g)}g^{(2)}\varepsilon(g^{(1)})=g
we have:

\displaystyle|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}\overset{C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{i,j}}{\mapsto}\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|g^{(1)}h\rangle_{j}

\displaystyle\overset{C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}^{-1}_{i,j}}{\mapsto}\sum_{(g)}|g^{(3)}\rangle_{i}|S(g^{(2)})g^{(1)}h\rangle_{j}

(101)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|\varepsilon(g^{(1)})1_{{\cal A}}h\rangle_{j}=|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}.

To establish
C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{i,j}^{-1}\circ C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{i,j}=\operatorname{id}_{{\cal A}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{{\cal A}}
, we only require the following Hopf algebra axioms:
S(g^{(1)})g^{(2)}=\varepsilon(g)1_{{\cal A}}
and
\sum_{(g)}g^{(2)}\varepsilon(g^{(1)})=g
.

Remark 3.1
.

Notice that the above construction does not hold for weak Hopf algebras
Böhm et al. (1999)
, as
\sum_{(g)}g^{(1)}S(g^{(2)})\neq\varepsilon(g)
in this case. However, since we won’t use the inevitability of local gates, our construction of Hopf cluster state also works for weak Hopf case.
A better way of generalization to the weak Hopf cluster state requires using the equivalence between the cluster state model and the quantum double model, which will be established in Sec.
7
.

Total space

\mathcal{H}_{tot}=\otimes_{v\in V}\mathcal{H}_{v}
,
\mathcal{H}_{v}={\cal A}

Z-preferred state

|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle
,
Z|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle=|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle

X-preferred state

|\mathbb{1}\rangle=\sqrt{|{\cal A}|}|\lambda\rangle
,
X|\mathbb{1}\rangle=|\mathbb{1}\rangle

Preferred product state

|\Omega\rangle=(\otimes_{v_{o}}|\lambda\rangle_{v_{o}})\otimes(\otimes_{v_{e}}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle_{v_{e}})

Edge entangler

\begin{aligned} C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{i,j}:|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}\mapsto\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|g^{(1)}h\rangle_{j}\\
C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{i,j}^{-1}:|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}\mapsto\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|S(g^{(1)})h\rangle_{j}\\
C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{i,j}:|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}\mapsto\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|hS(g^{(1)})\rangle_{j}\\
C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{i,j}^{-1}:|g\rangle_{i}|h\rangle_{j}\mapsto\sum_{(g)}|g^{(2)}\rangle_{i}|hg^{(1)}\rangle_{j}\end{aligned}

Table 2:
Summary of preferred states and edge entangler operations for Hopf cluster state.

3.2
CSS-type Hopf cluster state

Now, let’s construct the CSS-type Hopf cluster state.
The input state of the cluster circuit is the preferred product state

|\Omega\rangle=(\otimes_{v_{o}}|\mathbb{1}\rangle_{v_{o}})\otimes(\otimes_{v_{e}}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle_{v_{e}}).

(102)

Recall that we have assumed a global ordering of the edge set. Denoting them with subscripts, we have
e_{1},\cdots,e_{|E|}
.
Since each edge is directed and the cluster graph is bipartite, there are two possible configurations of the edges:

•

If the edge is directed from an odd vertex to an even vertex, we set
U_{e}=C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}
:

\displaystyle\hbox to65.84pt{\vbox to22.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.46773pt\lower-4.46773pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

(103)

•

If the edge is directed from an even vertex to an odd vertex, we set
U_{e}=C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}
:

(104)

In this manner, the CSS-type Hopf cluster state for a given cluster graph
K
and Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}
is defined as

|K,\mathcal{A}\rangle=U_{e_{|E|}}\cdots U_{e_{1}}|\Omega\rangle.

(105)

We will also call
U_{E}=U_{e_{|E|}}\cdots U_{e_{1}}
(where
E
represents the edge set of graph
K
) the circuit operations and
|\Omega\rangle
the initial state.

Proposition 6
.

For the edge entangler operations, we have the following observations:

1.

Notice that if the distance between two edges
e,e^{\prime}
is greater or equal to two, viz., they are not connected via some vertex, then the edge entangler operations are commutative:
[U_{e},U_{e^{\prime}}]
.

2.

For each odd vertex
v_{o}
with input state
h
, the local ordering
e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}
yields

\sum_{(h)}|h^{(n+1)}\rangle_{v_{o}}\otimes X^{\leftrightarrow}_{h^{(n)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{\leftrightarrow}_{h^{(1)}},

(106)

where
X^{\leftrightarrow}
is chosen as
\overset{\rightarrow}{X}
or
\overset{\leftarrow}{X}
depending on the orientations of each edge.
When
{\cal A}
is cocommutative (
\Delta=\tau\circ\Delta
), these edge operators will commute with each other, as is the case for the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[G]
. In this scenario, there is no need for a local ordering of edges for each odd vertex.

3.

For each even vertex
v_{e}
, if
e
is an input edge and
e^{\prime}
is an output edge,

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.5} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

then
[U_{e},U_{e^{\prime}}]
vanishes.
For two input edges (or output edges), the edge entangler operators do not commute in general.

Proof.

1. This is because for
d(e,e^{\prime})\geq 2
,
U_{e}
and
U_{e^{\prime}}
have support on different Hopf qudits.
2. It’s a direct consequence of the definition of edge entangler operations.
3. Since
[\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{v},\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{w}]=0
for any
w,v\in V
, we have
[C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{e},C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{e^{\prime}}]=0
.
∎

The third statement of Proposition
6
is crucial: if the even vertex
v_{o}
has more than two bonds (
|N_{E}(v_{e})|>2
), the commutativity cannot be reached in general. There must be a local ordering to make the definition of the Hopf cluster state well-defined.

Proposition 7
.

For a general cluster graph, the circuit operator
U_{E}=U_{e_{|E|}}\cdots U_{e_{1}}
in general cannot be decomposed into the composition of some commutative local gates.

Proof.

Notice that, to divide the edge set into some disjoint union of subsets
E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup E_{m}
such that
[U_{E_{i}},U_{E_{j}}]=0
, we can only divide the edge set along the even vertex, since the edges connected to an odd vertex are not commutative in general. Based on the third statement of Proposition
6
, we must divide
N_{E}(v_{e})
into the input edge set
N_{E}^{i}(v_{e})
and the output edge set
N_{E}^{o}(v_{e})
, and then cut the edge set of the graph along the even vertex in this way to satisfy the locality condition. However, when
|N_{E}^{i}(v_{e})|\geq 2
, this will introduce more than two odd vertices, all the edges connected to these vertices must be put into the same set since they are not commutative. These odd vertices will introduce more even vertices that connect to them. Repeating the dividing along these new even vertices will introduce some other odd vertices, eventually, the size of the local set can reach the size of the total edge set
E
.
∎

Although we cannot have locality condition for the general graph, when the bonds of all even vertices are two and
|N_{E}^{i}(v_{e})|=|N_{E}^{o}(v_{e})|=1
, we can make the local stabilizers commute with each other.
The details will be illustrated in Sec.
4
, which is crucial for discussing SPT phases of the Hopf cluster states
Jia ()
.

Remark 3.2
.

Notice that our construction of Hopf cluster state is based on controlled left regular actions. We can introduce the controlled right regular actions

C\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{i,j}|h\rangle\otimes|g\rangle=|h^{(2)}\rangle\otimes|gh^{(1)}\rangle,\quad C\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{i,j}|h\rangle\otimes|g\rangle=|h^{(2)}\rangle\otimes|S(h^{(1)})g\rangle.

(107)

By setting the same preferred initial state
|\Omega\rangle
and edge entangler as the above operators, we can also construct a Hopf cluster state. All the properties we discussed in this section, and will discuss in the sequel, also hold for this construction.

3.3
Stabilizers and non-invertible symmetry

The stabilizer group for the qubit state is a subgroup of the Pauli group for which the code state is invariant under the action of the stabilizers. Here we will adopt a more general definition of stabilizers, which are the operators that leave the state of our interest invariant.

For the Hopf cluster state, the stabilizers can be constructed in a similar way as that for the qubit cluster state.
Recall that for Haar integral
\lambda=S(\lambda)
which implies
\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{\lambda}
. Set this operator as
X=\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{\lambda}
we have
X|\mathbb{1}\rangle=|\mathbb{1}\rangle
.
For each odd vertex
v_{o}
and
X(v_{o})
, the stabilizer is of the form

T(v_{o})=U_{e_{|E|}}\cdots U_{e_{1}}X(v_{o})U_{e_{1}}^{-1}\cdots U_{e_{|E|}}^{-1}.

(108)

It’s clear that for Hopf cluster state
|K,{\cal A}\rangle
, we have
T(v_{o})|K,{\cal A}\rangle=|K,{\cal A}\rangle
.
Similarly, for the even vertex
v_{e}
, the operator
Z(v_{e})
in Eq. (
45
) leaves
|1_{\mathcal{A}}\rangle_{v_{e}}
invariant.
The corresponding stabilizer is defined as

Q(v_{e})=U_{e_{|E|}}\cdots U_{e_{1}}Z(v_{e})U_{e_{1}}^{-1}\cdots U_{e_{|E|}}^{-1}.

(109)

It’s easy to verify
Q(v_{e})|K,{\cal A}\rangle=|K,{\cal A}\rangle
.

For odd vertex, we can also introduce

\overset{\rightarrow}{T}_{g}(v_{o})=U_{e_{|E|}}\cdots U_{e_{1}}\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g}(v_{o})U_{e_{1}}^{-1}\cdots U_{e_{|E|}}^{-1},g\in{\cal A}.

(110)

\overset{\leftarrow}{T}_{g}(v_{o})=U_{e_{|E|}}\cdots U_{e_{1}}\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g}(v_{o})U_{e_{1}}^{-1}\cdots U_{e_{|E|}}^{-1}.

(111)

They form representations of Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
in the sense that

\overset{\rightarrow}{T}_{1_{{\cal A}}}=I,\quad\overset{\rightarrow}{T}_{g}\overset{\rightarrow}{T}_{h}=\overset{\rightarrow}{T}_{gh},

(112)

similarly for
\overset{\leftarrow}{T}_{g}(v_{o})
.
From
g\lambda=\varepsilon(g)\lambda=\lambda S(g)
, it’s clear that

\overset{\rightarrow}{T}_{g}|K,{\cal A}\rangle=\varepsilon(g)|K,{\cal A}\rangle=\overset{\leftarrow}{T}_{g}|K,{\cal A}\rangle.

(113)

To summarize, we have:

Proposition 8
.

The Hopf cluster state supports two Hopf
{\cal A}
-symmetries on each odd vertex, which are defined by Eqs. (
110
) and (
111
). Similarly, it also supports two Hopf
{\cal A}^{\rm op}
-symmetries, if we replace the left regular action with the right regular action.

For even vertex and
\Gamma\in\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
, we introduce

Q_{\Gamma}(v_{e})=U_{e_{|E|}}\cdots U_{e_{1}}J_{\Gamma}(v_{e})U_{e_{1}}^{-1}\cdots U_{e_{|E|}}^{-1}.

(114)

Then it’s easy to check that

Q_{\Gamma}(v_{e})|K,{\cal A}\rangle=d_{\Gamma}|K,{\cal A}\rangle.

(115)

And

Q_{\Gamma}(v_{e})Q_{\Phi}(v_{e})=Q_{\oplus_{\Psi}N_{\Gamma\Phi}^{\Psi}\Psi}(v_{e}).

(116)

From
J_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}
, we define

Q_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}(v_{e})=U_{e_{|E|}}\cdots U_{e_{1}}J_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}(v_{e})U_{e_{1}}^{-1}\cdots U_{e_{|E|}}^{-1}.

(117)

Similar results hold. For
E_{\Gamma}
and
E_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}
, we can construct two
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
-symmetries.
To summarize, we have:

Proposition 9
.

The Hopf cluster state supports two
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
-symmetries on each even vertex, which is defined by Eqs. (
114
) and (
117
). Similarly, it also supports two
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
-symmetries on each even vertex, if we replace
J_{\Gamma}
and
J_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}
with
\tilde{J}_{\Gamma}
and
\tilde{J}_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}
in Eqs. (
114
) and (
117
).

4
Hopf cluster-lattice state on
d
-dimensional spatial manifold

As discussed in the last section, to ensure a finite-depth circuit, we impose commutativity for local operations, which implies certain local structures of even vertex for the graph.
For our convenience, we introduce the following definition:

Definition 4

(Cluster lattice for a
d
-dimensional manifold)
.

For an arbitrary
d
-dimensional spatial manifold, a cluster lattice
\mathbb{M}^{d}
can be constructed as follows: We begin by cellulating the manifold, and then consider its
1
-dimensional skeleton as the lattice. The vertices of this lattice are referred to as odd vertices. Subsequently, we assign an orientation to each edge of the lattice and place even vertices along these edges. See Fig.
2
for an illustration.

Figure 2:
An example of cluster lattice on a torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. We first create a cellulation of
\mathbb{T}^{2}
and regard the vertices as odd vertices (depicted as red vertices in the figure). An orientation is assigned to each edge. Then, for each edge, we add an even vertex (represented as teal vertices in the figure). These even vertices are all bivalent. The original edge now becomes two edges connected to the even vertex, one input edge, and one output edge.

We will use the term ‘cluster lattice’ to distinguish it from the ‘cluster graph’. Note that a cluster lattice is a special case of a cluster graph.
As a graph
\mathbb{M}^{d}=(V,E)
, a cluster lattice is always bipartite. The even vertices are all bivalent, with one input edge and one output edge, while the odd vertices can connect to an arbitrary number of even vertices depending on the lattice structure.
We still need to introduce a local ordering of edges connected to an odd vertex. To simplify the notation, we still adopt a global ordering of all edges of the lattice, naturally inducing a local ordering for each vertex.
Also, notice that any given local ordering of
N_{E}(v_{o})
can be embedded into a global ordering of total edge set
E
.

Recall that there exists a local order of edges connecting to each odd vertex
v_{o}
, denoted as
e_{v_{o},1},\ldots,e_{v_{o},m}
. We set
U_{e_{v_{o},k}}=C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}
if
v_{o}
is the starting point of edge
e_{v_{o},k}
; otherwise, we set
U_{e_{v_{o},k}}=C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}
. We define the odd vertex operator as

W_{v_{o}}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}U_{e_{v_{o},j}},

(118)

where the order is crucial, since
[U_{e_{v_{o},j}},U_{e_{v_{o},l}}]\neq 0
in general.
However, for different odd vertices
v_{o}
and
w_{o}
, the corresponding entangler operators commute

[W_{v_{o}},W_{w_{o}}]=0.

(119)

For two odd vertices
v_{o}
and
w_{o}
that do not simultaneously connect to some even vertex, it’s clear that
[W_{v_{o}},W_{w_{o}}]=0
. If they connect to some even vertex
u_{e}
simultaneously via edges
e_{i}
and
e_{j}
, from the construction of the cluster lattice, we observe that
u_{e}
must not be the endpoint (or starting point) of two edges simultaneously. This implies that
[U_{e_{1}},U_{e_{2}}]=0
, as one arises from the
\overset{\rightarrow}{X}
and the other from the
\overset{\leftarrow}{X}
. Consequently,
[W_{v_{o}},W_{w_{o}}]=0
. This guarantees that the circuits have a locality structure.

Definition 5

(Hopf cluster-lattice state)
.

For a cluster lattice
\mathbb{M}^{d}
and a Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
, the Hopf cluster state is defined as

|\mathbb{M}^{d},{\cal A}\rangle=(\prod_{v_{o}}W_{v_{o}})|\Omega\rangle.

(120)

Using the global ordering of the edge set, it can be expressed as

|\mathbb{M}^{d},{\cal A}\rangle=(\prod_{j=1}^{|E|}U_{e_{j}})|\Omega\rangle.

(121)

It’s easy to verify that the two definitions are equivalent.

For a
1
-dimensional manifold, all vertices of a bipartite graph are bivalent. To ensure that the graph is a cluster lattice, we only need to impose some extra conditions on the orientation of each edge. This simplified the discussion of SPT phases in the 1d cluster state.
We believe our construction here for the Hopf cluster-lattice state provides a natural generalization of cluster states for discussing topological phases that have a Hopf symmetry. This provides a quite different construction from the anyonic chain
Feiguin et al. (2007)
and the model based on Hopf comodule algebra
Inamura (2022)
.
The detailed discussion from a topological phase perspective will be given in another work
Jia ()
.

5
Hopf tensor network representation

In this section, we will establish the tensor network representation for the Hopf cluster state.
Our construction of a tensor network is a combination of tensor network and string diagrams of Hopf algebra, which is a powerful tool when doing calculations. Notice that this should not be confused with the tensor network introduced in Refs.
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
;
Girelli et al. (2021)
in solving Hopf and weak Hopf quantum double models.
In this part, we will always fix a basis
{1_{\mathcal{A}}=g_{0},g_{1},\cdots,g_{|{\cal A}|-1}}
of the Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}
and all the contractions are assumed to be made for this basis if not specified.

5.1
Hopf algebra data as tensors

We assume the network is read upwards, thus a general element
h\in{\cal A}
can be represented
as

(122)

with

.
recall that the inner product of
{\cal A}
is given by Eq. (
27
),
c_{k}=\langle g_{k},h\rangle
.
The
*
-operation of
{\cal A}
is antilinear:
h^{*}=\sum_{k}c_{k}^{*}g^{*}_{k}
, where
c^{*}_{k}
is complex conjugation
c_{k}
and
g_{k}^{*}
is involution of
g_{k}
. From
\langle g_{k},g_{l}\rangle=\Lambda(g^{*}_{k}g_{l})=\delta_{k,l}
, we see
\langle s,t\rangle=\sum_{k}a_{k}^{*}b_{k}
with
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
the coefficients of
s
and
t
respectively.

(123)

We will utilize three distinct types of tensor legs: (i) virtual Hopf legs, represented as black, denoting contracted edges spanning the basis
{\cal A}
; (ii) physical Hopf legs, highlighted as blue, corresponding to physical degrees of freedom valued in
{\cal A}
; and (iii) virtual non-Hopf legs, denoted by cyan, taking values in a Hilbert space V (possibly a representation space of
{\cal A}
or additional ancillary spaces). These distinctions facilitate the clear representation and manipulation of tensors in our computations.

Algebra tensor.
—
The algebraic structure is characterized by the structure constants
A_{ab}^{c}
, where

\mu(g_{a}\otimes g_{b})=g_{a}g_{b}=\sum_{c}A_{ab}^{c}g_{c}.

(124)

We will use labeled lines to represent the given basis with the respective label, dotted lines to represent the identity element
g_{0}=1_{\mathcal{A}}
, and the lines connecting two tensors to represent the contraction of the corresponding indices. For more details about the tensor network, see Refs.
Orús (2014)
;
Cirac et al. (2021)
.
The unit axiom of algebra can be represented diagrammatically as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A_{0b}^{c}=A_{b0}^{c}=\delta_{b,c},\end{aligned}

(126)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hbox to39.77pt{\vbox to49.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.83301pt\lower-18.18614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}

(127)

The associativity can be represented as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sum_{d}A_{ab}^{d}A_{dc}^{e}=\sum_{d}A_{ad}^{e}A_{bc}^{d}.\end{aligned}

(129)

Notice that associativity allows us to define the algebra tensor
A_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}^{b}
with
n
input legs and one output leg without ambiguity. There are
\frac{(2n-2)!}{n!(n-1)!}
(the
(n-1)
-th Catalan number) equivalent ways to decompose
A_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}^{b}
into the contraction of basic algebra tensors.

Coalgebra tensor.
—
The coproduct structure is characterized by the structure constant
C^{bc}_{a}

\Delta(g_{a})=\sum_{b,c}C^{bc}_{a}g_{b}\otimes g_{c}.

(132)

The counit axiom can thus be represented as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sum_{b}\varepsilon_{b}C_{a}^{bc}=\sum_{c}\varepsilon_{d}A_{a}^{cd}=\delta_{a,c},\end{aligned}

(136)

The coassociativity allows us to define the coalgebra tensor
C^{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}_{b}
with
n
input legs and one output leg without ambiguity. There are also
\frac{(2n-2)!}{n!(n-1)!}
(the
(n-1)
-th Catalan number) equivalent ways to decompose
C^{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}_{b}
into the contraction of basic coalgebra tensors.

Bialgebra axioms.
— The definition of bialgebra requires that
\varepsilon
and
\Delta
must be algebra homomorphisms.
For counit tensor, we have
\varepsilon(g_{a}g_{b})=\varepsilon(g_{a})\varepsilon(g_{b})
for all
a,b
, which is equivalent to say
\sum_{c}A_{ab}^{c}\varepsilon_{c}=\varepsilon_{a}\varepsilon_{b}
.

(137)

For the coalgebra tensor, we have

\Delta(g_{a}g_{b})=\Delta(g_{a})\Delta(g_{b})
, which is equivalent to
\sum_{c}A_{ab}^{c}C_{c}^{de}=\sum_{i,j,k,l}C_{a}^{ij}C_{b}^{kl}A_{ik}^{d}A_{jl}^{e}
.
