(138)

These basic rules of tensor contraction can be used to derive more complicated rules, such as those for
\Delta(g_{a}g_{b}g_{c}\cdots)
.

Antipode tensor.
— For Hopf algebra, antipode can also be represented as a tensor
S(g_{a})=\sum_{b}S_{a}^{b}g_{b}
.
It satisfies
\sum_{b,c,d}C_{a}^{bc}S_{c}^{d}A_{bd}^{e}=\varepsilon_{a}\delta_{e,0}=\sum_{b,c,d}C_{a}^{bc}S_{b}^{d}A_{dc}^{e}
.

(139)

Using these structure tensors, given a Hopf quantum circuit with input as Hopf qudits and gates as Hopf operations, we can translate the corresponding string diagram into a tensor network, which we call a Hopf tensor network.

5.2
Hopf tensor network representation of Hopf cluster state

For the Hopf cluster state, there exists a Hopf tensor network representation. As we will see, our construction of the Hopf tensor network is a combination of tensor network and string diagrams, and it can naturally recover the matrix product state (MPS) and projected entangled pair states (PEPS) representations for the case
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{C}[G]

Brell (2015)
;
Fechisin et al. (2023)
. Many properties therein have a natural explanation using the Hopf algebra structure.

Hereinafter we will put Haar integral
|\lambda\rangle
instead of
|\mathbb{1}\rangle\propto|\lambda\rangle
on each odd vertex of a cluster graph for the simplicity of the discussion.
For a given Hopf cluster state
|K,{\cal A}\rangle
, we can draw a corresponding string diagram using the structure constant of
{\cal A}
, see Fig.
3
and Fig.
4
for an illustration, and this string diagram can be translated into a Hopf tensor network if we replace the corresponding morphisms with structure tensors.

For an odd vertex connecting
n-1
edges, the corresponding local tensor is an
n
-leg tensor with one physical leg and
n-1
virtual legs, it can be represented by

(S\otimes\operatorname{id}\otimes\cdots\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta_{n}(\lambda)=\begin{aligned} \hbox to92.86pt{\vbox to78.42pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.77766pt\lower-32.41252pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}.

(140)

The middle diagram represents the complete version of the odd vertex tensor, while the right one is a simplified version. In the middle diagram, we assume that all legs are directed upwards. In the right diagram, we need to emphasize the direction of the legs to determine if there is an antipode map.
The local ordering of the edges connecting to the odd vertex determines how we place the comultiplication components on each leg of the tensor.
If the gate operator for the edge connecting to even vertex is
C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}
, there will be an antipode tensor; otherwise, there is no
S
.
We’ve employed blue legs to denote the physical degrees of freedom and black lines to represent the virtual indices in
{\cal A}
. The blue leg always take the last comultiplication component
\lambda^{(n)}
.

We observe that the odd vertex tensor essentially serves as a comultiplication tensor.
It’s also clear that, to contract the odd vertex tensor with a nearby even vertex tensor, the local ordering of edges is crucial, we can not change the order of edges arbitrarily.
However, it is worth mentioning that for the Haar integral, we have
\tau\circ\Delta(\lambda)=\Delta(\lambda)
, this means

\sum_{(\lambda)}\lambda^{(1)}\otimes\lambda^{(2)}=\sum_{(\lambda)}\lambda^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(1)},

(141)

or equivalently,
\sum_{a}\lambda^{a}C_{a}^{b_{1}b_{2}}=\sum_{a}\lambda^{a}C_{a}^{b_{2}b_{1}}
.
This further implies

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda)}\lambda^{(1)}\otimes\lambda^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(3)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda)}\lambda^{(3)}\otimes\lambda^{(1)}\otimes\lambda^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda)}\lambda^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(3)}\otimes\lambda^{(1)},

(142)

and so on in a similar fashion. Thus, the odd vertex tensor exhibits a cyclic property. This implies that we can permute the indices according to the above rule before performing the contraction.

Figure 3:
Illustration of the Hopf tensor network representation of the Hopf cluster state. On the left, there is a cluster graph with a globally ordered edge set,
e_{1},\cdots,e_{12}
. For each vertex, the neighboring edges have a local ordering induced by global ordering of edge set. For an odd vertex, this local ordering determines how we place the comultiplication component on each edge and the edge direction determine whether we take antipode; for an even vertex, the local ordering determines the order for taking multiplication.
On the right, we present the Hopf tensor network for the corresponding Hopf cluster state, where
\lambda
represents Haar integral and
\mu
represnets the multiplication map.

For the even vertex
v_{e}
, the tensor is essentially a multiplication tensor,
the leg corresponding to input state
1_{{\cal A}}=g_{0}
can be removed under the multiplication. The even vertex tensor can be represented as

(143)

Notice that for even vertex tensor, we do not need to stress that the directions of legs, since this part of information is encoded in the odd vertex tensor, all virtual legs of even vertex tensor are contracted with an odd vertex tensor.
We can alternatively encode the information of the antipode map in the even vertex: If the edge is an input edge, no antipode tensor is needed; otherwise, we contract the corresponding input line with an antipode tensor. This simply reflects how we handle the legs connecting even and odd vertices (See Fig.
4
).
However, the local ordering of edges connecting to the even vertex is still crucial here. If there are
n
odd vertices connected to this even vertex, the corresponding even vertex tensor is given by
A_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}^{\prime}}^{b}S_{a_{n}}^{a^{\prime}_{n}}
. It’s clear that the local ordering for
N_{E}(v_{e})
is also necessary, as multiplication is not generally commutative.

The operations on Hopf qudits can also be represented using Hopf tensor networks.

(146)

This implies
Z_{\Gamma}Z_{\Phi}=Z_{\Gamma\otimes\Phi}
. In a similar way, we can show
\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}\tilde{Z}_{\Phi}=\tilde{Z}_{\Phi\otimes\Gamma}
.
By employing the Hopf tensor network, we have a more intuitive approach to handling quantum states and operations for Hopf qudits

Notice that our proposal for the Hopf tensor network representation differs from the matrix product operator approach discussed in Refs.
Molnar et al. (2022)
;
Garre-Rubio et al. (2023)
. However, the method of matrix product operator algebras can also be applied to 1d Hopf qudit chains.
The tensor networks based on Hopf algebras discussed in Refs.
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
;
Girelli et al. (2021)
are actually special cases of the Hopf tensor network described here (if we regard the function
\varphi\in\bar{{\cal A}}
as a special tensor
f(g_{k})=f_{k}
). A more general form will be discussed in Sec.
9
.
Since the Hopf tensor network in Refs.
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Girelli et al. (2021)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
can be used to solve the Hopf quantum double model, it can also be employed to solve the Hopf cluster state model based on the correspondence between the Hopf cluster state and the Hopf quantum double model (See Sec.
7
).

Figure 4:
The Hopf tensor network for an one dimensional Hopf cluster state.

5.3
Example: 1d cluster lattice case

Let us take (1+1)D Hopf cluster lattice state as an example. The basic building block of the Hopf tensor network is odd vertex tensor and even vertex tensor, see Fig.
4
for a depiction.

. For odd vertex, the tensor is given by

(147)

This is given by first taking the comultiplication of the Haar integral
\Delta_{2}(\lambda)
, then acting the antipode
S
to the first component
\lambda^{(1)}
. This antipode reflects the fact that for the corresponding edge, we apply the edge gate
C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}
.

The even vertex tensor is simply a multiplication tensor with two input legs, notice that since we assign
1_{{\cal A}}
to the even vertex, it won’t contribute to the multiplication, thus we omit this dotted leg. Diagrammatically, we have

(149)

where we use the prime symbol to distinguish the component obtained from the comultiplication of Haar integral on different odd vertices.

Let us now derive the local relations for the action of Hopf Pauli-X and Pauli-Z on odd and even vertex tensors. These results will be crucial tools for studying the symmetry of the 1d Hopf cluster chain.
Notice that for Haar integral of
{\cal A}
, we have

\sum_{(\lambda)}S(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}x=\sum_{(\lambda)}xS(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}.

(150)

This further implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda)}S(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(3)}x=\sum_{(\lambda)}x^{(2)}S(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}S(x^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(3)}.

(151)

Substituting
x=S(h)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda)}S(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(3)}S(h)=\sum_{(\lambda)}S(h^{(1)})S(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}h^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(3)}.

(152)

This can be represented using the Hopf tensor network as

(153)

Since
S(\lambda)=\lambda
, substituting it into Eq. (
150
) and applying the twist map
\tau:w\otimes v\mapsto v\otimes w
, we have

\sum_{(\lambda)}S(\lambda^{(1)})x\otimes\lambda^{(2)}=\sum_{(\lambda)}S(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes x\lambda^{(2)}.

(154)

This further implies that

\sum_{(\lambda)}S(\lambda^{(1)})x^{(2)}\otimes S(x^{(1)})\lambda^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(3)}=\sum_{(\lambda)}S(\lambda^{(1)})\otimes\lambda^{(2)}\otimes x\lambda^{(3)}.

(155)

(156)

Using the facts
\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{x}={\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{S(x)}
and
\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{x}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{S^{-1}(x)}
, we can obtain expressions for the action of
\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}
and
\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}
on odd tensors similar to Eqs. (
153
) and (
156
).

(158)

For
\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{x}={\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{S(x)}
and
\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{x}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{S^{-1}(x)}
, similar expressions exist.

(164)

Using
\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}=Z_{\Gamma(S^{-1}(\cdot))}
and
\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}=Z_{\Gamma(S(\cdot))}
, we obtain similar expressions for the actions of
\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}
and
\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}
on odd and even vertex tensors.

6
One dimensional Hopf cluster state model

In this section, we delve into the one-dimensional Hopf cluster state, a lattice theory that exhibits fusion category symmetry in (1+1)D. Distinct from anyonic chains, the Hopf cluster state model possesses a tensor product structure for its total Hilbert space, rendering it advantageous for the discussion of SPT phases in the context of quantum matter.
As we will see in Sec.
7
, the (1+1)D Hopf cluster state model can be regarded as a boundary theory of the (2+1)D Hopf quantum double model
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
.
This also sheds new light on the connection between the SPT phase and the topological phase: the categorical symmetry of
d
dimensional SPT phase is related to the
d+1
dimension topological order
Kong et al. (2020)
. These topics will be discussed in detail in Ref.
Jia ()
.

6.1
Hopf cluster state lattice Hamiltonian

Let us now consider a 1d cluster lattice
\mathbb{M}^{1}
, which is depicted in Fig.
4
. We will label the vertices as
1,\cdots,2L
.
The preferred state is
|\Omega\rangle=|\lambda\rangle_{1}|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle_{2}|\lambda\rangle_{3}\cdots|1_{{\cal A}}\rangle_{2L}
.
For periodic boundary conditions, we assume that all edges of the cluster graph are directed from left to right. For an odd vertex
v_{o}
, the local ordering of the edge set
N_{E}(v_{o})
is assumed to be
\{e_{1}=e_{L},e_{2}=e_{R}\}
, where
e_{L}
and
e_{R}
are the edges connecting to
v_{o}
from the left and right sides, respectively.
The entangler operator is then given by

U_{E}=\prod_{i:\text{odd}}C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{i,i+1}C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{i,i-1},

(165)

which means
W_{v_{i}}=C\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{i,i+1}C\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{i,i-1}
in Eq. (
120
).
Notice that different choices of edge orientations and the local ordering of the edge set
N_{E}(v_{o})
result in different models, but their physical properties are the same. This will be discussed later in Sec.
7
.

For odd vertex
i
, we define X-type local stabilizer operator as (
\lambda
is Haar integral)

\mathfrak{A}_{i}=\sum_{(\lambda)}\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{\lambda^{(1)}}(i-1)\otimes\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda^{(3)}}(i)\otimes\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda^{(2)}}(i+1).

(166)

We can similarly define
\mathfrak{A}^{h}
for
h\in{\cal A}
.
For even vertex
j
, we define the Z-type operator

\mathfrak{B}^{\Gamma}_{j}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}[Z^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}(j-1)\otimes Z_{\Gamma}(j)\otimes Z_{\Gamma}(j+1)]

(167)

and

\mathfrak{B}_{j}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}\mathfrak{B}^{\Gamma}_{j}.

(168)

As will be explained in Sec.
7
,
\mathfrak{B}^{\Gamma}_{j}
is defined based on the character function
\chi_{\Gamma}:{\cal A}\to\mathbb{C}
which is an element in the dual Hopf algebra
\bar{{\cal A}}
. This can also be generalized to a general element
\psi\in\bar{{\cal A}}
. We can define
\mathfrak{B}^{\psi}
using the comultiplication structure of
\bar{{\cal A}}
.

Remark 6.1
.

Notice that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
’s and
\mathfrak{B}_{j}
’s bear a resemblance to the vertex and face operators
A_{v}
and
B_{f}
in the quantum double model
Kitaev (2003)
;
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Yan et al. (2022)
;
Jia et al. (2024b)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
.
We can prove their equivalence, see Sec.
7
.
Refer to Fig.
5
, folding the 1d cluster lattice into a zigzag configuration yields a 2d (quasi-1d) lattice. In the quantum double lattice construction, Hopf qudits are positioned on the edges. To ensure a properly defined quantum double lattice, an ancillary vertex (seen as the bottom vertex in Fig.
5
) is introduced.
We’ll term this lattice the virtual lattice, with its associated vertices and faces named virtual vertices and virtual faces, respectively.
Given that the directions of each virtual edge are chosen as downwards and rightwards, and the edges surrounding the faces and vertices are assumed to be clockwise, we assert that the corresponding vertex operator
A_{v}
is equivalent to the odd-site operator
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
defined earlier. Similarly, the face operator
B_{f}
corresponds to the odd-site operator
\mathfrak{B}_{j}
defined previously. See Sec.
7
for a detailed discussion.

Figure 5:
Illustration depicting the transformation from local stabilizers for the Hopf cluster state into the face and vertex operators of the Hopf quantum double model. By folding the 1d cluster lattice into a zigzag configuration, a 2d lattice is formed, represented by gray dotted lines in the figure, with vertices depicted as stars.

Theorem 1
.

The Hopf cluster state lattice Hamiltonian

H_{\rm cluster}=-\sum_{i:\rm odd}\mathfrak{A}_{i}-\sum_{j:\rm even}\mathfrak{B}_{j}

(169)

is a local commutative projector (LCP) Hamiltonian, viz.,
\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathfrak{B}_{j}
are projectors and
[\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathfrak{B}_{j}]=0
for all
i\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
and even
j\in 2\mathbb{N}
,
[\mathfrak{A}_{k},\mathfrak{A}_{l}]=0
and
[\mathfrak{B}_{s},\mathfrak{B}_{t}]=0
for all
k,l\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
and
s,t\in 2\mathbb{N}
.
The Hopf cluster state
|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle
is the stabilizer state of
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{j}

\mathfrak{A}_{i}|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle=\mathfrak{B}_{j}|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle=|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle

(170)

for all odd
i
and even
j
.

Proof.

We first prove that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
’s are projectors. Recall that the inner product of Hopf qudit
{\cal A}
is given by Eq. (
27
),
\Lambda(x^{*}gy)=\Lambda((g^{*}x)^{*}y)
implies that

\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g}^{\dagger}=\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{g^{*}}.

(171)

Using Eq. (
19
) and cocomutativity of
\Lambda
, we have
\Lambda(x^{*}yS(g))=\Lambda(S(g)x^{*}y)=\Lambda((xS(g)^{*})^{*}y)=\Lambda((xS(g^{*}))^{*}y)
, which implies

\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g}^{\dagger}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{g^{*}}.

(172)

By definition of
\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{\lambda}
, we see

(\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{\lambda})^{\dagger}=\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{\lambda^{*}}.

(173)

Since
\lambda^{*}=\lambda
, we have
(\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{\lambda})^{\dagger}=\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{\lambda}
, viz.,
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
’s are Hermitian.
It’s clear that
\mathfrak{A}^{x}_{i}\mathfrak{A}^{y}_{i}=\mathfrak{A}^{xy}_{i}
. From
\lambda^{2}=\lambda
, we have
(\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{\lambda})^{2}=\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{\lambda}
, meaning that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
’s are projectors.

Directly proving
\mathfrak{B}^{\dagger}=\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{2}=\mathfrak{B}
is challenging. However, leveraging Proposition
13
in Appendix, we obtain
\mathfrak{B}=B_{f}^{\Lambda}
. In references
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
, it’s shown that
B_{f}^{\Lambda}
are Hermitian projectors. Hence,
\mathfrak{B}_{j}
are projectors for all
j\in 2\mathbb{N}
.

Using Proposition
13
in the next section, the commutativity of
[\mathfrak{A}_{k},\mathfrak{A}_{l}]=[\mathfrak{B}_{s},\mathfrak{B}_{t}]=[\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathfrak{B}_{j}]=0
can be proved in a similar way as that in Refs.
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
.
Notice that the character
\chi_{\Gamma}
is cocommutative (i.e.
\chi_{\Gamma}(xy)=\chi_{\Gamma}(yx)
), which implies a stronger relation

[\mathfrak{A}_{i},\mathfrak{B}_{j}^{\Gamma}]=0.

(174)

Here we will provide an alternative proof of above equation based on the Hopf tensor network representation of the Hopf cluster state.
For a 1d lattice with
2L
vertices, the total space is
{\cal A}^{\otimes 2L}
. For product state
|\Omega(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2L})\rangle=|x_{i}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|x_{2L}\rangle
, which applying the cluster circuit
U_{E}
in Eq. (
165
), we obtain a state
|\Psi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2L})\rangle
. Since
U_{E}
is invertible, its necessary to show that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\mathfrak{B}_{j}^{\Gamma}|\Psi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2L})\rangle=\mathfrak{B}_{j}^{\Gamma}\mathfrak{A}_{i}|\Psi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2L})\rangle
for all such
|\Psi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2L})\rangle
.
The only non-trivial case is when
j=i\pm 1
, let’s assume
j=i+1
without loss of generality.
We have the following Hopf tensor network representation of
|\Psi(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2L})\rangle
:

\displaystyle\hbox to303.32pt{\vbox to87.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.321pt\lower-10.15863pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.5} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{218.03728pt}{65.01366pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 301.7 89.96)} \pgfsys@hbox{59}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.5} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.5} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{246.49004pt}{25.17998pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 341.07 34.84)} \pgfsys@hbox{59}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(175)

When applying
\mathfrak{A}_{i+2}
, from
\lambda x_{i+2}=\varepsilon(x_{i+2})\lambda
, we have

\mathfrak{A}_{i+2}|\Psi(\cdots,x_{i},x_{i+1},x_{i+2},x_{i+3},x_{i+4},\cdots)\rangle=\varepsilon(x_{i+2})|\Psi(\cdots,x_{i},x_{i+1},\lambda,x_{i+3},x_{i+4},\cdots)\rangle

(176)

Applying
\mathfrak{B}_{i+1}^{\Gamma}
on
|\Psi(\cdots,x_{i},x_{i+1},x_{i+2},x_{i+3},x_{i+4},\cdots)\rangle
gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\Gamma[S(x_{i}^{(4)})x_{i}^{(3)}x_{i+1}^{(2)}S(x_{i+2}^{(1)})x_{i+2}^{(5)}]|x_{i}^{(1)},x_{i}^{(5)},x_{i}^{(2)}x_{i+1}^{(1)}S(x_{i+2}^{(2)}),x_{i+2}^{(4)},x_{i+2}^{(3)}x_{i+3}S(x_{1+4}^{(1)}),x_{i+4}^{(3)},x_{i+4}^{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\Gamma[x_{i+1}^{(2)}S(x_{i+2}^{(1)})x_{i+2}^{(5)}]|x_{i}^{(1)},x_{i}^{(3)},x_{i}^{(2)}x_{i+1}^{(1)}S(x_{i+2}^{(2)}),x_{i+2}^{(4)},x_{i+2}^{(3)}x_{i+3}S(x_{1+4}^{(1)}),x_{i+4}^{(3)},x_{i+4}^{(2)}\rangle

(177)

where we omit the sum symbol for comultiplication to avoid cluttering the equations.
Combining the fact that

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda)}\lambda^{(1)}\otimes\lambda^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(3)}\otimes\lambda^{(4)}\otimes\lambda^{(5)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda)}\lambda^{(2)}\otimes\lambda^{(3)}\otimes\lambda^{(4)}\otimes\lambda^{(5)}\otimes\lambda^{(1)}

(178)

and Eqs. (
176
) and (
177
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{i+1}^{\Gamma}\mathfrak{A}_{1+2}|\Psi(\cdots,x_{i},x_{i+1},x_{i+2},x_{i+3},x_{i+4},\cdots)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x_{i+2})\mathfrak{B}_{i+1}^{\Gamma}|\Psi(\cdots,x_{i},x_{i+1},\lambda,x_{i+3},x_{i+4},\cdots)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\Gamma[x_{i+1}^{(2)}]\varepsilon(x_{i+2})|\Psi(\cdots,x_{i},x_{i+1}^{(1)},x_{i+2},x_{i+3},x_{i+4},\cdots)\rangle

(179)

In a similar way, we can prove that, applying
\mathfrak{A}_{1+2}\mathfrak{B}_{i+1}^{\Gamma}
on
|\Psi(\cdots,x_{i},x_{i+1},x_{i+2},x_{i+3},x_{i+4},\cdots)\rangle
gives the same result.

Notice that Eqs. (
176
) and (
177
) also implies that
|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle
is invariant under
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{j}
. Since
\varepsilon(\lambda)=1
, we have

\mathfrak{A}_{i}|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle=\varepsilon(\lambda)|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle=|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle.

(180)

Combining Eqs. (
177
) and (
178
), we have

\mathfrak{B}_{j}|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}\Gamma(1_{{\cal A}})|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle=d_{\Gamma}|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle.

(181)

This, together with the Peter-Weyl formula for Hopf algebra, further implies that
\mathfrak{B}_{j}|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle=|\mathbb{M}^{1},{\cal A}\rangle
.
∎

6.2
Non-invertible symmetries

The Hopf cluster state Hamiltonian in Eq. (
169
) has two non-invertible global symmetries characterized by
{\cal A}
and
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
for the open boundary condition.
Notice that the symmetry
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
is in fact the Grothendieck ring
\operatorname{Gr}(\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev})
of
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
, which has the basis as characters for irreducible representations of
{\cal A}
. Since character
\chi_{\Gamma}
is in the dual Hopf algebra
\bar{{\cal A}}
, we have an embedding

\operatorname{Gr}(\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev})\hookrightarrow\bar{{\cal A}}^{\rm op},

(182)

where superscript ‘
op
’ means that we take opposite algebra, viz.,
a\cdot^{\rm op}b:=b\cdot a
.
This implies that the symmetry algebra can be embedded into
{\cal A}\times\bar{{\cal A}}
.

Let us first consider the Hopf
{\cal A}
-symmetry, which is defined as an operator acting on all odd vertices:

F_{h}=\sum_{(h)}\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{h^{(1)}}(1)\otimes\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{h^{(2)}}(3)\otimes\cdots\otimes\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{h^{(L)}}(2L-1).

(183)

It’s clear that

F_{g}F_{h}=F_{gh},\quad F_{1_{{\cal A}}}=I.

(184)

Notice that this symmetry is not the same as on-site group symmetry, where
U_{g}=\otimes_{j}U_{g}^{(j)}
and each local site carries the same group element
g
. Here, for different vertices, we assign a different component of the comultiplication of
h\in{\cal A}
.

Proposition 10
.

The Hopf cluster state Hamiltonian
H_{\rm cluster}
in Eq. (
169
) commutes with
F_{g}
for all
g\in{\cal A}
, thus it has a Hopf
{\cal A}
-symmetry.

Proof.

Notice that
\mathfrak{A}
, when acting on Hopf qudit for odd vertices, is a action from the left-hand side, while
F_{g}
acts from the right-hand side, thus
[F_{g},\mathfrak{A}]=0
.
To prove
[\mathfrak{B},F_{g}]=0
, consider Hopf tensor network in Eq. (
175
), applying
F_{g}
, we replace
x^{(3)}_{i}
with
x^{(3)}S(g^{(\frac{i+1}{2})})
and
x^{(3)}_{i+2}
with
x^{(3)}_{i+2}S(g^{(\frac{i+1}{2}+1)})
. Then applying
B^{\Gamma}_{i+2}
gives the factor

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\Gamma[S^{2}(g^{(\frac{i+1}{2}+1)})S(x^{(4)}_{i})\cdots x_{i+2}^{(5)}S(g^{(\frac{i+1}{2}+2)})]

(185)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\Gamma[S(g^{(\frac{i+1}{2}+2)})g^{(\frac{i+1}{2}+1)}S(x^{(4)}_{i})\cdots x_{i+2}^{(5)}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(g^{(\frac{i+1}{2}+1)})\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\Gamma[S(x^{(4)}_{i})\cdots x_{i+2}^{(5)}].

This implies that
[\mathfrak{B}_{i+1}^{\Gamma},F_{g}]=0
, leading us to the required result.
∎

Consider an open cluster chain with two odd vertices at the ends
1
1

1

For other cases of open chains that may have one odd vertex and one even vertex, or two even vertices at the ends, the analysis is similar.
, when acting
F_{g}
on the corresponding Hopf cluster state, it is equivalent to acting
\sum_{(g)}\tilde{\overset{\leftarrow}{X}}_{g^{(1)}}\otimes\tilde{\overset{\rightarrow}{X}}_{g^{(2)}}
on virtual legs at two ends. This can be derived using Eq. (
153
). Represented as Hopf tensor network, we obtain (the
\sum_{(g)}
for comultiplication has be omitted):

(186)

where
x,y\in{\cal A}
are boundary elements that we choose to take the inner product with dangling legs at two ends of the chain.

We can also introduce a non-invertible global
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
-symmetry that acts on all even vertices:

D_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}[\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}(2)\otimes\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}(4)\otimes\cdots\otimes\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}(2L)].

(187)

They satisfy a fusion rule

D_{\Gamma}D_{\Phi}=D_{\Phi\otimes\Gamma},

(188)

which means that they form a
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
symmetry.
Note that this differs from the definition in Ref.
Fechisin et al. (2023)
due to the cocommutative nature of the group algebra
\Delta(g)=g\otimes g
for
g\in G
. Thus, they use
Z_{\Gamma}
to construct the global symmetry. However, for a general Hopf algebra, this is not the case, since

Z_{\Gamma}Z_{\Phi}|x\rangle=\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}\otimes\Gamma(x^{(2)})\otimes\Phi(x^{(3)})\neq\sum_{(x)}x^{(1)}\otimes\Phi(x^{(2)})\otimes\Gamma(x^{(3)})=Z_{\Phi}Z_{\Gamma}|x\rangle.

(189)

The operator
\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}[Z_{\Gamma}(2)\otimes Z_{\Gamma}(4)\otimes\cdots\otimes Z_{\Gamma}(2L)]
does not commute with
\mathfrak{B}
, and therefore is no longer a symmetry operator.

Proposition 11
.

The Hopf cluster state Hamiltonian
H_{\rm cluster}
in Eq. (
169
) commutes with
D_{\Gamma}
for all
\Gamma\in\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
, thus it has a
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\rm rev}
-symmetry.

Proof.

The commutativity relation
[\mathfrak{B}^{\Phi}_{2k},D_{\Gamma}]
is clear from the definition. For odd vertex of
\mathfrak{B}^{\Phi}=Z_{\Phi}^{\ddagger}(2k-1)\otimes Z_{\Phi}(2k)\otimes Z_{\Phi}(2k+1)
, the corresponding operator is
Z_{\Phi}(2k)
. We have
[Z_{\Phi}(2k),\tilde{Z}_{\Gamma}(2k)]=0
, which implies
[\mathfrak{B}^{\Phi}(2k),D_{\Gamma}]=0
and further
[\mathfrak{B}(2k),D_{\Gamma}]=0
.

Consider an odd vertex,
\mathfrak{A}_{2k+1}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{\lambda^{(1)}}\otimes\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda^{(3)}}\otimes\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{\lambda^{(2)}}
. For the product state
|\cdots,x_{2k},x_{2k+1},x_{2k+2},\cdots\rangle
, we have

\begin{aligned} &D^{\Gamma}\mathfrak{A}_{2k+1}|\cdots,x_{2k},x_{2k+1},x_{2k+2},\cdots\rangle\\
=&\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\Gamma(\cdots x_{2k}^{(1)}S(\lambda^{(2)})\lambda^{(3)}x_{2k}^{(1)}\cdots)|\cdots,x_{2k}^{(2)}S(\lambda^{(1)}),\lambda^{(5)}x_{2k+1},\lambda^{(4)}x_{2k+2}^{(2)},\cdots\rangle\\
=&\varepsilon(\lambda^{(2)})\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\Gamma(\cdots x_{2k}^{(1)}x_{2k}^{(1)}\cdots)|\cdots,x_{2k}^{(2)}S(\lambda^{(1)}),\lambda^{(4)}x_{2k+1},\lambda^{(3)}x_{2k+2}^{(2)},\cdots\rangle\\
=&\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\Gamma(\cdots x_{2k}^{(1)}x_{2k}^{(1)}\cdots)|\cdots,x_{2k}^{(2)}S(\lambda^{(1)}),\lambda^{(3)}x_{2k+1},\lambda^{(2)}x_{2k+2}^{(2)},\cdots\rangle\\
=&\mathfrak{A}_{2k+1}D^{\Gamma}|\cdots,x_{2k},x_{2k+1},x_{2k+2},\cdots\rangle\end{aligned}.

(190)

This completes the proof.
∎

Similar to
{\cal A}
-symmetry, the bulk symmetry operators can also be pushed to boundaries. A Hopf Z-string operator (Eq. (
187
)) can be effectively regarded as two boundary Z-operators
\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}\tilde{Z}^{\ddagger}_{\Gamma}\otimes Z_{\Gamma}^{\ddagger}
.
This can be derived based on Eqs. (
159
)-(
164
), if we take open boundary condition for Z-string operator(
\alpha
and
\beta
as left and right indices), represented as Hopf tensor network, we have

(191)

where we use cyan dot to represent the free indices in representation space.

Using the embedding of
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\mathrm{rev}}
into
\bar{{\cal A}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, we see that the
Z
-string operator
D_{\Gamma}
in Eq. (
187
) can be regarded as taking the comultiplication
\Delta_{L}(\chi_{\Gamma})
(where
L
is the number of even vertices) of
\chi_{\Gamma}
and assigning the component
\chi_{\Gamma}^{(i)}
to the
i
-th even vertex. This implies that the categorical symmetry
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})^{\mathrm{rev}}
is a special case of Hopf symmetry, and this is a general phenomenon when we only consider the fusion structure of the category.

7
Correspondence between 1d Hopf cluster state model and quasi-1d Hopf quantum double model

As mentioned before, the 1d cluster state model is intricately connected to Kitaev’s quantum double model. In this section, we establish this connection by mapping a 1d cluster state lattice Hamiltonian into a quasi-1d quantum double model for Hopf qudits. This correspondence can assist us in providing a weak Hopf generalization of the 1d cluster state model based on our previous work
Jia et al. (2023b)
.

Qubit case.
—
It’s worthwhile to start by discussing the qubit case, where the quantum double model is commonly known as the toric code model
Kitaev (2003)
.
For quantum double lattice, the vertex operators and face operators are of the forms:

A_{v}=\prod_{j\in\partial v}X_{j},\quad B_{f}=\prod_{j\in\partial f}Z_{j},

(192)

where
v,f
are vertices and faces of the quantum double lattice.
There are two kinds of formalisms for the quantum double lattice. In edge-lattice one, as described in Ref.
Kitaev (2003)
, the qudit is placed on the edges, allowing discussion of vertices and faces. The other formalism is the chessboard representation, commonly used in the quantum error-correction community.
As shown in Fig.
5
, the edge-lattice one draw as a gray dotted lattice, where the vertices are represented as star symbols.
The chessboard representation is given by light green and light orange triangles.

The lattice Hamiltonian reads

H=-\sum_{v}A_{v}-\sum_{f}B_{f}.

(193)

Consider a quasi-1d quantum double lattice, composed of vertex and face plaquettes arranged in a sequence, represented using a chessboard layout as shown in Fig.
6
.
In the case of periodic boundary conditions (Fig.
6
), when viewing the lattice as a 2d disk, it becomes apparent that the vertex plaquette operator surrounded by all face plaquettes can be expressed as a product of all vertex plaquette operators. Consequently, we can eliminate this particular vertex plaquette from the lattice. More precisely, consider a bicycle-wheel-like quantum double lattice on a disk. The boundary vertex operator is given by
A_{v}=X_{e_{1}}X_{e_{2}}X_{s_{3}}
, where the
X_{e_{1}}
and
X_{e_{2}}
operators act on the "tire" edges, and the
X_{s_{3}}
operator acts on the "spoke" edge. In contrast, the bulk vertex operator is
A_{u}=X_{s_{1}}X_{s_{2}}\cdots X_{s_{n}}
, where all
X_{s_{i}}
operators act on the "spoke" edges. We observe that
\prod_{v:\text{tire}}A_{v}=A_{u}
. Here, for each "tire" edge, the
X
operator acts twice, effectively becoming the identity. This implies that there is no need to impose an additional
A_{u}=1
condition to obtain the ground state. Furthermore, note that removing the bulk vertex operator introduces a rough boundary at the center of the disk
Jia et al. (2024b)
.

Figure 6:
Illustration depicting the quasi-1d toric code model represented in a chess-board layout: (a) periodic boundary conditions and (b) open boundary conditions. The vertex plaquettes are depicted as light green triangles, while the face plaquettes are represented as light orange triangles. The red circles represent odd vertices, while the teal squares denote even vertices in the original Hopf cluster lattice.

Via folding the 1d cluster state model into a zigzag form (Fig.
5
), we see that the cluster state model in Eq. (
4
) is completely the same with the quasi-1d toric code model. To summarize, we have

Proposition 12
.

The 1d qubit cluster state model is equivalent to a quasi-1d toric code model.

It’s noteworthy to mention that the cluster state Hamiltonian in Eq. (
2
) is equivalent to the quasi-1d toric code model expressed in Wen’s formulation
Wen (2003)
.
Notice that the global symmetries of the cluster state model can be expressed

F=\otimes_{i:\rm odd}X_{i},\quad D=\otimes_{j:\rm even}Z_{j}.

(194)

We see that in the quantum double model, this is just the closed string operator,
F
is a closed magnetic string and
D
is a closed electric string.

Hopf qudit case.
—
In the case of finite groups and more general Hopf algebras, establishing this equivalence is not straightforward, as the vertex and face operators appear to differ significantly from those of the quantum double model. However, as we will observe, they are still equivalent, akin to the qubit case.

To proceed with establishing the equivalence between the 1d Hopf cluster state model and the quasi-1d Hopf quantum double model, let’s first revisit the definition of local stabilizers in the Hopf quantum double model.

The vertex operators are constructed using left regular actions:
L_{+}^{h}=\overset{\rightarrow}{X}_{h}
and
L_{-}^{h}=\overset{\leftarrow}{X}_{h}
. We have adopted the notation
L_{\pm}
following Kitaev’s original work
Kitaev (2003)
to enhance clarity in the discussion.
The edge operator are constructed from
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Yan et al. (2022)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
:

\displaystyle T_{+}^{\varphi}|x\rangle=

\displaystyle|\varphi\rightharpoonup x\rangle=|\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi,x^{(2)}\rangle x^{(1)}\rangle,

(195)

\displaystyle T_{-}^{\varphi}|x\rangle=

\displaystyle|x\leftharpoonup\bar{S}(\varphi)\rangle=|\sum_{(x)}\langle\bar{S}(\varphi),x^{(1)}\rangle x^{(2)}\rangle=|\sum_{(x)}\langle\varphi,S(x^{(1)})\rangle x^{(2)}\rangle,

(196)

where
\varphi\in\bar{{\cal A}}
is linear functional over
{\cal A}
and we have used Sweedler’s arrow notation. Refer to our previous work
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
for details about the notation we have used.

Let
j
be a directed edge with endpoint
v
. We define
L^{h}(j,v)
as follows: if
v
is the origin of
j
, then
L^{h}(j,v)=L_{+}^{h}(j)
; otherwise,
L^{h}(j,v)=L_{-}^{h}(j)
.
For edge
j
surrounding face
f
, if
f
is on the left hand side, we choose
T^{\varphi}(j,f)=T_{-}^{\varphi}(j)
, otherwise, we choose
T^{\varphi}(j,f)=T_{+}^{\varphi}(j)
.
For our purposes, for a given site
s=(v,f)
, we order the edges around the vertex
v
and the face
f
clockwise with the origin
s
, which is different from that in Refs.
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
.
This is also the reason that we choose to use the above convention.
The vertex operators and face operators on a site are defined as

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(198)

the corresponding quantum double vertex and face operators are defined as

\displaystyle A^{h}(s)=\sum_{(h)}L_{-}^{h^{(1)}}(j_{1})\otimes L_{+}^{h^{(2)}}(j_{6})\otimes L_{+}^{h^{(3)}}(j_{5})\otimes L_{-}^{h^{(4)}}(j_{4}),

(199)

\displaystyle B^{\varphi}(s)=\sum_{(\varphi)}T_{-}^{\varphi^{(1)}}(j_{4})\otimes T_{-}^{\varphi^{(2)}}(j_{3})\otimes T_{+}^{\varphi^{(3)}}(j_{2})\otimes T_{+}^{\varphi^{(4)}}(j_{1}).

The local stabilizers for the quantum double model are denoted as
A_{v}=A^{\lambda}_{v}
and
B_{f}=B^{\Lambda}_{f}
, where
\lambda\in\mathcal{A}
and
\Lambda\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}
are Haar integrals. Because of the cocommutativity of Haar integrals, their definition is independent of the initial site, relying solely on the vertex and face, respectively.

The equivalence between the 1d Hopf cluster state model and the quasi-1d quantum double model can be summarized as follows:

Proposition 13
.

By folding the 1D cluster lattice into a zigzag configuration (Fig.
5
), the 1d Hopf cluster state Hamiltonian aligns with the quasi-1d Hopf quantum double Hamiltonian, where the odd vertex and even vertex operators correspond to the vertex and face operators of the quantum double model, respectively:

\mathfrak{A}_{2k+1}=A_{v}^{\lambda},\quad\mathfrak{B}_{2k}=B_{f}^{\Lambda}.

(200)

If we look at the quantum double lattice, this means that the Hopf cluster state model is equivalent to a quantum double model with a rough boundary and a smooth boundary defined on a ladder (Fig.
7
).

Proof.

By folding the 1D cluster lattice into a zigzag configuration (Fig.
5
), we observe that the odd vertex operator
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
corresponds to the vertex operator
A_{v}^{\lambda}
by definition.
For even vertex operators
\mathfrak{B}_{j}
’s, the proof is more complicated.
Notice that, for
\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})
, when acting
\mathfrak{B}^{\Gamma}_{j}
on
x_{j-1},x_{j},x_{j+1}
, we have

\mathfrak{B}^{\Gamma}_{j}|x_{j-1},x_{j},x_{j+1}\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}^{\prime}[\Gamma(S(x_{j-1}^{(1)})x_{j}^{(2)}x_{j+1}^{(2)})]|x_{j-1}^{(2)},x_{j}^{(1)},x_{j+1}^{(1)}\rangle.

(201)

Using the character
\chi_{\Gamma}\in\bar{{\cal A}}
, we see

\mathfrak{B}^{\Gamma}_{j}|x_{j-1},x_{j},x_{j+1}\rangle=\sum_{\chi_{\Gamma}}{\chi_{\Gamma}^{(1)}}(S(x_{j-1}^{(1)}))\chi_{\Gamma}^{(2)}(x_{j}^{(2)})\chi_{\Gamma}^{(3)}(x_{j+1}^{(2)})|x_{j-1}^{(2)},x_{j}^{(1)},x_{j+1}^{(1)}\rangle.

(202)

This means that

\mathfrak{B}^{\Gamma}_{j}=B^{\chi_{\Gamma}}.

(203)

Therefore

\mathfrak{B}_{j}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\operatorname{Irr}({\cal A})}\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{|{\cal A}|}B^{\chi_{\Gamma}}.

(204)

From Eq. (
31
), we obtain

\mathfrak{B}^{\Gamma}_{j}=B_{f}^{\Lambda}.

(205)

This completes the proof.
∎

We have demonstrated that the 1d cluster state model is equivalent to the quantum double model with a smooth boundary, where the internal vertex operator for the 2d bulk is omitted.
In this case, the boundary data is chosen to be identical to that of the bulk
Jia et al. (2024b)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
.
The global symmetries of the Hopf cluster state model are also equivalent to closed ribbon operators of the quasi-1d Hopf quantum double model.
Since we have removed the internal vertex operator for a bicycle-wheel lattice (take periodic boundary condition for the gray lattice in Fig.
5
) and focus solely on the face operators and vertex operators on the boundary, this implies that we must first perform some coarse-graining (entanglement renormalization
Buerschaper et al. (2013b)
) on the bulk to map the bulk lattice into a bicycle-wheel lattice and then project the bulk to obtain the cluster state model
Albert et al. (2021)
.

There is another way to interpret the correspondence between the cluster state model and quantum double model. As shown in Fig.
6
, we can regard the cluster state model as a quantum double model on a very thin ribbon whose one boundary (teal) is smooth, while the other boundary (red) is rough
Jia et al. (2024b)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
.
Also, note that the result in this section asserts that the cluster state model is a specific choice of boundary conditions for the quantum double model.
A natural question arises: What model corresponds to other boundary conditions of the Hopf quantum double model? This question can be addressed by employing SymTFT or topological holography
Jia (2024)
.

8
SymTFT and Hopf ladder model

In the previous section, we show that the Hopf cluster state model can be regarded as a quasi-1d Hopf quantum double model with a smooth boundary and a rough boundary.
In fact, this point of view can be generalized by considering general boundary conditions in the framework of SymTFT. The SymTFT (also called topological holography) provides a general framework for us to understand the fusion category symmetry for both gapped and gapless phases
Kong et al. (2020)
;
Schäfer-Nameki (2024)
;
Huang and Cheng (2023)
;
Bhardwaj et al. (2024c)
;
Freed et al. (2024)
;
Gaiotto and Kulp (2021)
;
Bhardwaj and Schafer-Nameki (2023)
;
Apruzzi et al. (2023)
;
Bhardwaj et al. (2024d)
;
Zhang and Córdova (2024)
;
Ji and Wen (2020)
.
A thorough discussion on the application of SymTFT to Hopf and weak Hopf lattice gauge theory will be presented in our forthcoming work
Jia (2024)
. Here, we only summarize some key results.

The SymTFT is a triple
(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym}),\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym},\mathcal{B}_{\rm phys})
that forms a sandwich, where: (i)
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym})
is a 2d topological phase; (ii)
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym}
is called the symmetry boundary and characterizes the fusion category symmetry of the (1+1)D phase. The symmetry boundary must be a gapped topological boundary; (iii) The physical boundary
\mathcal{B}_{\rm phys}
need not be gapped or topological.
The SymTFT for (1+1)D phase is defined on a sandwich manifold
\Sigma\times[0,1]
with
\operatorname{dim}\Sigma=1
.
In this section, we only consider the case that
\Sigma
is closed, i.e., it is a circle.
We place
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym})
in the bulk
\Sigma\times(0,1)
, and position the two boundaries
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\rm phys}
on
\Sigma\times\{0\}
and
\Sigma\times\{1\}
, respectively. After compactification over the interval
[0,1]
, we obtain a (1+1)D theory.

To understand the Hopf cluster state model in the SymTFT framework, consider the fusion category symmetry given by a fusion category
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym}=\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
for some Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
. The corresponding SymTFT consists of the following data:

•

For the (2+1)D bulk, we put a Hopf lattice gauge theory whose topological excitation is given by the representation category
\mathsf{Rep}(D({\cal A}))
of the quantum double
D({\cal A})
, notice that
\mathsf{Rep}(D({\cal A}))
is braided monoidal equivalent to the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym})
of the multifusion symmetry
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym}\simeq\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
.

•

The symmetry boundary is a topological boundary condition
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym}=\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
of the bulk lattice gauge phase
\mathsf{Rep}(D({\cal A}))
, which corresponds to a smooth boundary for which all magnetic fluxes are condensed
Jia et al. (2023a)
. This topological boundary condition
can be characterized in at least three different ways: (i) A Lagrangian algebra
L_{m}\in\mathsf{Rep}(D({\cal A}))
which is a superposition of all magnetic fluxes; (ii) A module category
\mathcal{M}=\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
over
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
; (iii) A comodule algebra
K={\cal A}
over Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
.

•

The physical boundary condition
\mathcal{B}_{\rm phys}
is chosen as a rough boundary. This is also a gapped topological boundary condition, meaning that after compactification, the resulting (1+1)D phase is gapped. The Lagrangian algebra
L_{e}
of this boundary is a superposition of all electric charges. The module category is
\mathcal{N}=\mathsf{Vect}
. The comodule algebra is trivial comodule algebra
\mathbb{C}
.

•

The local operators are generated by Wilson lines that can end at both boundaries. For our Hopf lattice gauge theory, the Wilson lines are ribbon operators connecting the two boundaries. Since the two boundaries we choose are fully electric-condensate boundary and a fully magnetic-condensate boundary, there is only one Wilson operator that can connect both boundaries, which is the one that carries the trivial topological charge. This guarantees the ground state space is non-degenerate:

\text{GSD}=\dim\operatorname{Hom}(L_{m}\otimes L_{e},\mathbf{1})=1,

(206)

where
\mathbf{1}
is the vacuum charge in the bulk.
The fusion category symmetry acts on local operators via half-braiding, which is realized in quantum double mode via half-braiding ribbon operators
Jia et al. (2023a)
.
Since the only Wilson line carries the vacuum charge, this implies that the fusion category symmetry
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sym}=\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
is fully preserved, with no symmetry breaking.

Notice that since both of two boundaries are gapped, we can choose any one of them as the symmetry boundary. We have chosen smooth boundary as symmetry boundary and see the there is a
\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})
symmetry. We can also choose the rough boundary as the symmetry boundary, in this case the symmetry is give by
\mathsf{Rep}(\bar{{\cal A}})
, the representation category of dual Hopf algebras. Since each object in
\mathsf{Rep}(\bar{{\cal A}})
can be regarded as an elements in
{\cal A}
, we have an embedding

\mathsf{Fun}_{\mathsf{Rep}({\cal A})}(\mathsf{Vect},\mathsf{Vect})\simeq\mathsf{Rep}(\bar{{\cal A}})\hookrightarrow{\cal A}.

(207)

All these symmetry elements are cocommutative due to the fact that character functions are cocommutative.
This matches well with our analysis of the Hopf symmetry of the cluster state model using the explicit lattice construction: on a closed manifold, the symmetry element must be cocommutative.

Now let us consider the general case, where we choose two different general boundaries for the Hopf quantum double model. One boundary is given by the
{\cal A}
-comodule algebra
K
, and the other is given by the
{\cal A}
-comodule algebra
J
.
For the bulk
\Sigma\times(0,1)
, we assign a Hopf algebra
{\cal A}
.
On the symmetry boundary
\Sigma\times\{0\}
, we assign a Hopf comodule algebra
K
. Depending of the orientation of the boundary, we can choose left or right comodule algebras as input data
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
.
Similarly, on the physical boundary
\Sigma\times\{1\}
, we assign a Hopf comodule algebra
J
.
The compactification over the interval
[0,1]
can be realized by considering an ultra-thin sandwich lattice, which is a ladder lattice denoted by
\mathbb{M}^{1}
. See Figure
7
for a depiction. The edges for symmetry boundary are drawn in red, the edges for physical boundary are drawn in blue, and the bulk edge is drawn in black.

Figure 7:
Depiction of the ladder lattice of SymTFT sandwich for Hopf lattice gauge theory.

There are three types of local operators: the face operator
\mathfrak{B}_{f}
, which is defined using the Haar measure of
{\cal A}
; the red vertex operator
\mathfrak{A}_{v}
; and the blue vertex operator
\mathfrak{A}_{w}
, both of which are defined using the symmetric separability idempotent of the comodule algebra. The explicit expressions are given in Refs.
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
. The Hamiltonian is defined as

H_{\rm ladder}=-\sum_{f}\mathfrak{B}_{f}-\sum_{v:~\text{s.b.}}\mathfrak{A}^{K}_{v}-\sum_{w:~\text{p.b.}}\mathfrak{A}^{J}_{w}.

(208)

This model will be called the Hopf ladder model
Jia (2024)
. The Hopf cluster state model is a special case of this model with
K=\mathbb{C}
and
J={\cal A}
, as we have pointed out. Also notice that the Hopf ladder model can be solved exactly using the Hopf tensor network
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
.
If we choose the two boundaries to be the same,
J=K
, we obtain the symmetry breaking phase, in which the fusion category symmetry
\mathcal{C}={{}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}^{H}

Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
is fully broken.
In this case, the ground state space is degenerate, and the degeneracy is determined by the number of fusion channels between the anyon-condensates of the two boundaries.
To ensure that the model gives an SPT phase protected by
\mathcal{C}={{}_{K}}\mathsf{Mod}_{K}^{H}
, we need to require that there are no nontrivial fusion channels between the two boundary anyon-condensates. The existence of nontrivial fusion channels will partially or fully break the fusion category symmetry
Huang and Cheng (2023)
;
Jia (2024)
.

9
Graph and hypergraph states from the pairing of weak Hopf algebras

In the previous sections, our main focus was to construct the Hopf graph state based on a bipartite graph by generalizing the CSS-type qubit graph state (Eq. (
3
)). In this part, we will consider the general hypergraph, of which a graph is a special case, and discuss how to construct the Hopf hypergraph state based on the decorated hypergraph by generalizing Eq. (
1
).

A hypergraph
G=(V,E)
consists of a vertex set
V=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\}
, and a hyperedge set
E=\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{m}\}\subset 2^{V}
(i.e., a
k
-hyperedge is a set
e_{i}=\{v_{i_{1}},\cdots,v_{i_{k}}\}\subset V
).
A
k
-uniform hypergraph is a hypergraph whose each hyperedge contains precisely
k
vertices. A graph is
2
-uniform hypergraph.
We can also introduce the direction of the
k
-hyperedge, which is an ordered
k
-tuple
e=(v_{i_{1}},\cdots,v_{i_{k}})
. For a
k
-hyperedge, there are
k!
possible choices of hyperedge directions.
The hyperedges of a multi-hypergraph are allowed to appear repeatedly in the hyperedge set, the number of copies of a hyperedge
e
is called multiplicity of
e
and is denoted as
m_{e}
.
A hypergraph whose all hyperedges are directed is called a directed hypergraph.
A hypergraph is called connected if, for any two vertices, there is a set of hyperedges (a hyper-path) that connect them.
If a hypergraph is not connected, it can be broken into several connected components, therefore, it suffices to investigate connected hypergraph only.

9.1
Generalized hypergraph state for qudit

In order to construct the quantum state from a given directed hypergraph, we assign a corresponding Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{v_{i}}
to each vertex
v_{i}
(e.g., for discrete variable case,
\mathcal{H}_{v_{i}}=\mathbb{C}^{d}
; for continuous variable case,
\mathcal{H}_{v_{i}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
). To each
k
-hyperedge
e_{i}=\{v_{i_{1}},\cdots,v_{i_{k}}\}
,
we assign a
k
-linear functional
\phi_{e_{i}}:\otimes_{j}\mathcal{H}_{v_{i_{j}}}\to\mathbb{C}
. By definition,
\phi_{e_{i}}
is determined by the values
\phi_{e_{i}}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})
over basis elements
I=(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}_{d_{1}}\times\cdots\mathbb{Z}_{d_{k}}
.
In this way, we obtain a decorated hypergraph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E,\{\mathcal{H}_{v_{i}}\},\{\phi_{e_{i}}\})
.
The corresponding state
|\mathcal{G}\rangle
can be constructed using a quantum circuit that will be discussed later.
Notice that for hyperedge with no direction,
\phi_{e}
needs to be symmetric for all variables. More precisely, for a
k
-hyperedge,
\phi_{e}(i_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,i_{\sigma(k)})=\phi_{e_{i}}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})
holds for arbitrary
k
-permutation
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(I)\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{k}
.

Consider a
k
-hyperedge
e
, we can introduce the hyperedge gate
U_{e}
to realize the edge function
\phi_{e}
(to ensure the unitarity of
U_{e}
, here we choose
\phi_{e}=e^{i\theta_{e}}
with
\theta_{e}
a real function, a more general case will be discussed in the sequel):

\displaystyle U_{e}|I\rangle=\phi_{e}(I)|I\rangle=e^{i\theta_{e}(I)}|I\rangle.

(209)

This
U_{e}
is in fact a controlled operation

\displaystyle U_{e}=\sum_{I_{e\setminus\{v_{n}\}}}|I_{e\setminus\{v_{n}\}}\rangle\langle I_{e\setminus\{v_{n}\}}|\otimes U_{I_{e\setminus\{v_{n}\}}},

(210)

where
U_{I_{e\setminus\{v_{n}\}}}
is a diagonal unitary matrix with diagonal entries equal to
e^{i\theta_{e}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n-1},0)}
,
\cdots
,
e^{i\theta_{e}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{n-1},d_{n}-1)}
.
For the edge with no direction, using the permutation symmetry of the hyperedge function, there will also be a corresponding symmetry of the hyperedge gate, namely, it does not depend on the choice of target vertex on which the controlled-operation acts.
For instance, consider the qubit case, if
e
is a 2-hyperedge (viz., an edge),
if
e
is not directed, then
\theta_{e}(i,j)=\theta_{e}(j,i)
,

\displaystyle U_{e}=

\displaystyle|0\rangle\langle 0|\otimes\operatorname{diag}(e^{ia},1)+|1\rangle\langle 1|\otimes\operatorname{diag}(1,e^{ib})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{diag}(e^{ia},1)\otimes|0\rangle\langle 0|+\operatorname{diag}(1,e^{ib})\otimes|1\rangle\langle 1|,

(211)

where
a,b
are two independent real parameters and we have omitted the overall phase factor. If we take
a=0
and
b=\pi
,
U_{e}
becomes the controlled-
Z
gate.

There are several crucial classes of hyperedge gates, one prototypical class is the controlled power gate.
Choose a single-particle gate
V
and set
C^{0}(V)=V
, we define recursively

C^{k}(V)=C[C^{k-1}(V)]=\sum_{q}|q\rangle\langle q|\otimes(C^{k-1}(V))^{q}.

(212)

It’s easy to check that

C^{k}(Z)=\sum_{q_{1},\cdots,q_{k}}|q_{1}\cdots q_{k}\rangle\langle q_{1}\cdots q_{k}|\otimes V^{q_{1}\times\cdots\times q_{k}}.

(213)

Examples of controlled power gates include the following:

•

For qubit, the controlled-
Z
gate
C(Z)
and
k
-controlled-
Z
gate
C^{k}(Z)
(with
C^{0}(Z)=Z
).

•

For qudit, using the Heisenberg-Weyl operator
Z_{d}=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}}w_{d}^{q}|q\rangle\langle q|
with
w_{d}
the
d
-th root of unity, we have
C(Z_{d})
and
C^{k}(Z_{d})
(with
C^{0}(Z_{d})=Z_{d}
).

•

For fixed diagonal qudit unitary gate
P_{\theta}=\operatorname{diag}(1,e^{i\theta},\cdots,e^{i(d-1)\theta})
,
C^{k}(P_{\theta})
does not depend on the choice of target vertex. Setting
\theta=2\pi/d
, the
k
-controlled-
Z_{d}
gate is obtained.

Another gate we need is the generalized Hadamard gate
Cui et al. (2015)

H|0\rangle=|h\rangle,

(214)

where
h\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
is a image of
|0\rangle
.
For qubit case,
H
is just Hadamard gate.
We can now give the definition of the hypergraph state

Definition 6
.

For an arbitrary decorated hypergraph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E,\{\mathcal{H}_{v_{i}}\},\{\phi_{e_{i}}\})
, there is a corresponding generalized hypergraph state defined as

|\mathcal{G}\rangle=(\prod_{e\in E}U_{e}^{m_{e}})(\otimes_{v\in V}H_{v})|0\rangle^{\otimes|V|},

(215)

where
H_{v}
’s are generalized Hadamard gates acting on each vertex
v
and
U_{e}
’s are hyperedge gates determined by hyperedge function
\phi_{e}
, and
m_{e}
is the hyperedge multiplicity.
Note that, hyperedge unitary operators
U_{e}
’s commute with each other in general, thus we need not to specify the order in which these gates are applied.

The general procedure for finding stabilizers of a given generalized hypergraph state
|\mathcal{G}\rangle
is as follows: firstly, find the stabilizers of state
|h\rangle^{\otimes|V|}
, this can be done by choosing local operators
K_{v}
whose
+1
eigenstate is integral state
|h\rangle
, i.e.,
K_{v}=|h\rangle\langle h|\oplus A
(Notice that
A
is required to be a normal operator
[A,A^{\dagger}]=0
with all eigenvalues unequal to
+1
). Then, using the trick of conjugation operation we can obtain the stabilizers of the hypergraph state
T_{v}=(\prod_{e}U_{e})K_{v}(\prod_{e}U_{e})^{\dagger}
.

9.2
Weak Hopf hypergraph state

The above construction of generalized hypergraph states can be extended to Hopf qudits. It’s worth noting that all constructions in this section also apply to weak Hopf algebras.

To use the comultiplication here, we need a local ordering structure around each vertex
v
.
Suppose there are
n=|N_{E}(v)|
hyperedges connected to
v
, we need to introduce and ordering of the set
\{v\}\cup N_{E}(v)
. For example, consider a vertex
v
connected to four hyperedges
e_{1},\cdots,e_{4}
, we need to order
\{v,e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\}
:

\begin{aligned} \hbox to85.64pt{\vbox to87.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.82167pt\lower-43.54388pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

(216)

where we use the red line to denote
v
, the black line to denote the hyperedges, the ordering in this case is
(e_{1},v,e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
.
If we put
h\in{\cal A}
on the vertex
v
, we introduce comultiplication
\Delta_{|N_{E}(v)|+1}(h)
and put the respective component according to the local ordering to the set
\{v\}\cup N_{e}(v)
. We denote it as

h\mapsto\sum_{(h)}h^{(v)}\otimes h^{(e_{v,1})}\otimes\cdots\otimes h^{(e_{v,|N_{E}(v)|})}.

(217)

E.g., for the example in Eq. (
216
),
h^{(v)}=h^{(2)}
,
h^{e_{2}}=h^{(3)}
, and so on.

For the directed hyperedge
e
, we denote the ordered vertices in
e
as
v_{e,1},\cdots v_{e,m}
. For example, consider a 3-hyperedge

(218)

there is an ordering of the vertices inside the edge, the edge function
\psi_{e}:{\cal A}^{\otimes m}\to\mathbb{C}
acts on the elements of the corresponding vertices according to this ordering of
N_{V}(e)
.

Definition 7

((Weak) Hopf hypergraph state)
.

Consider a directed hypergraph
\mathcal{G}
with local ordering around each vertex, for a given (weak) Hopf algebra, we put
h_{v}\in{\cal A}
for each vertex
v
and put a linear function
\psi_{e}:{\cal A}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\cal A}\to\mathbb{C}
.
The corresponding (weak) Hopf hypergraph state is given by

|\mathcal{G}\rangle=\sum_{(h_{v}),v\in V}[\prod_{e\in E}\psi_{e}(h^{e}_{v_{e,1}},\cdots,h^{(e)}_{v_{e,m}})]\bigotimes_{v\in V}|h_{v}^{(v)}\rangle.

(219)

A crucial class of the edge function is defined by choosing

\phi\in\bar{{\cal A}}
, and define

\psi_{e}(h^{(e)}_{v_{e,1}},\cdots,h^{(e)}_{v_{e,m}})=\phi(h^{(e)}_{v_{e,1}}\cdots h^{(e)}_{v_{e,m}}).

(220)

In other words, we first multiply
h^{(e)}_{v_{e,1}},\cdots,h^{(e)}_{v_{e,m}}
and then apply
\phi
.
This can be equivalently regarded as taking the comultiplication of
\phi
and putting the corresponding component
\phi^{(i)}
to the vertices
v_{e,i}
.
Then using the pairing between
{\cal A}
and
\bar{{\cal A}}
, we have

\phi(h^{(e)}_{v_{e,1}}\cdots h^{(e)}_{v_{e,m}})=\sum_{(\phi)}\phi^{(1)}(h^{(e)}_{v_{e,1}})\cdots\phi^{(m)}(h^{(e)}_{v_{e,m}}).

(221)

The (weak) Hopf quantum double state
Buerschaper et al. (2013a)
;
Girelli et al. (2021)
;
Jia et al. (2023a)
;
Jia et al. (2023b)
is a special case of our (weak) Hopf hypergraph state with
\phi
chosen as Haar integral in
\bar{{\cal A}}
and the local ordering satisfy some other constraints. On a square lattice, it’s a
4
-uniform hypergraph state.

10
Discussion

In this paper, we propose a general construction of the Hopf cluster state. We also construct the lattice Hamiltonian for the 1d Hopf cluster state and discuss its non-invertible symmetries. This state is a potential candidate for the 1d SPT phase, differing from both the anyonic chain construction
Feiguin et al. (2007)
and the construction based on Hopf comodule algebras
Inamura (2022)
.
The discussion of the SPT phase for this model will be given in our future work
Jia ()
.
The connection we have established between the Hopf cluster state model and the quasi-1d quantum double model is also of significant interest.

Despite the progress that has been made, there are still some open problems. As mentioned, our construction does not apply to weak Hopf algebras. However, in investigating fusion category symmetry, it’s essential to consider weak Hopf algebras. This is because any given fusion category symmetry can be viewed as a representation category of some weak Hopf algebra. Notably, there exist fusion categories that cannot be represented as the representation category of Hopf algebras. We leave the solution to this problem for future study
Jia (2024)
, leveraging the correspondence between the 1d cluster state model and the quasi-1d quantum double model. The application of our model in MQBC can be implemented in a similar way as that in Ref.
Fechisin et al. (2023)
. This direction will also be discussed in our future work.
